范围与问题
尽管有充分记录的社会经济劣势,但之前的审查尚未综合有关罗马尼亚罗姆人健康状况的证据。
证据基础与方法
使用 Joanna Briggs Institute 方法和 PRISMA-ScR 指南,我们对 PubMed/MEDLINE、Web of Science、Science Direct 进行了系统检索,并辅以灰色文献和参考文献列表筛选,查找 2010-2025 年以英语或罗马尼亚语发表的研究。
共识与结论
慢性病负担显着高于普通人群:高血压 患病率 为 33-67%,2 型糖尿病为 10-29%,肥胖为 32-62%,有证据表明发病更早,并发症更严重,包括更容易进展为慢性肾病和终末期肾病。
争议与缺口
罗姆人社区的预期寿命差距为 3.5-4.6 年,预防性护理的接受率明显较低(普通人群为 39%,普通人群为 71%),而没有保险的比例很高(42-59%)。
科研与实践启示
在交叉的社会经济、结构和行为决定因素的推动下,罗马尼亚的罗姆人社区在几乎所有检查领域的健康状况都明显较差。

Overview of Health Status among Roma Population in Romania: A Scoping Review

Authors: David Valentin Mangaloiu, Eugenia Claudia Bratu, Carmen Madalina Raris-Fierbinteanu, Florentina Furtunescu
Original abstract (English)
Background: Roma are widely recognized as Europe ‘s largest ethnic minority and in-dependent estimates suggest that up to two million people live in Romania. Despite well-documented socioeconomic disadvantage, no prior review has synthesized evi-dence on the health status of Roma in Romania. Objectives: This scoping review mapped the literature on healthcare access, quality of life, chronic disease burden and communicable disease exposure among Roma communities in Romania. Methods: Us-ing the Joanna Briggs Institute methodology and PRISMA-ScR guidelines, we con-ducted a systematic search of PubMed/MEDLINE, Web of Science, Science Direct and supplemented by grey literature and reference-list screening, for studies published 2010–2025 in English or Romanian. Results: Thirty-nine sources (35 studies and 4 in-stitutional reports) met the inclusion criteria. Roma communities experience a life-expectancy gap of 3.5–4.6 years, markedly lower preventive-care uptake (39% vs 71% in the general population), and high rates of being uninsured (42–59%). Chronic disease burden was substantially higher than in the general population: hypertension prevalence ranged 33–67%, type 2 diabetes from 10–29%, and obesity from 32–62%, with evidence of earlier onset and more severe complications, including higher pro-gression to chronic kidney disease and end-stage renal disease. Health-related quality of life and mental health indicators were uniformly worse among Roma. Evidence on communicable diseases was sparse but suggested elevated hepatitis B/C prevalence and greater severity of measles and COVID-19. Conclusions: Roma communities in Romania experience markedly poorer health outcomes across nearly all domains ex-amined, driven by intersecting socioeconomic, structural and behavioral determinants.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Despite well-documented socioeconomic disadvantage, no prior review has synthesized evi-dence on the health status of Roma in Romania.
Evidence base and method
Us-ing the Joanna Briggs Institute methodology and PRISMA-ScR guidelines, we con-ducted a systematic search of PubMed/MEDLINE, Web of Science, Science Direct and supplemented by grey literature and reference-list screening, for studies published 2010–2025 in English or Romanian.
Consensus and conclusions
Chronic disease burden was substantially higher than in the general population: hypertension prevalence ranged 33–67%, type 2 diabetes from 10–29%, and obesity from 32–62%, with evidence of earlier onset and more severe complications, including higher pro-gression to chronic kidney disease and end-stage renal disease.
Controversies and gaps
Roma communities experience a life-expectancy gap of 3.5–4.6 years, markedly lower preventive-care uptake (39% vs 71% in the general population), and high rates of being uninsured (42–59%).
Research and practice implications
Roma communities in Romania experience markedly poorer health outcomes across nearly all domains ex-amined, driven by intersecting socioeconomic, structural and behavioral determinants.

数字时代缩小索马里童年时期的 疫苗接种 差距:错误信息、信任和获取的视角

Closing Somalia’s childhood vaccination gap in the digital era: a perspective on misinformation, trust, and access
作者： Abdisalam Abdullahi Mohamed
摘要中文翻译索马里的儿童免疫覆盖率有所提高,但 疫苗 剂量、人群和地点之间仍存在重要差距。世卫组织/联合国儿童基金会对 2025 年的最新估计报告称,首剂含白喉-破伤风-百日咳的 疫苗 (DTP1) 覆盖率为 77%,DTP3 为 72%,首剂含 麻疹 (MCV1) 的覆盖率为 61%,而 2020 年索马里健康和人口调查记录的覆盖率要低得多家庭调查覆盖范围。这些措施不能直接互换,它们说明了脆弱数据环境中的进展和持续的不确定性。本观点认为,错误信息、信任和获取应一起审查,同时认识到索马里有关数字 疫苗 错误信息的具体证据仍然有限。因此,既定的世卫组织 疫苗接种 (BeSD) 行为和社会驱动因素和世卫组织/联合国儿童基金会社会倾听指南被改编为索马里的操作途径,而不是作为新的行为理论提出。该改编规定了负责任的参与者、离线和数字收听渠道、语言和数据系统要求、服务链接规则、冲突设置的保障措施以及评估策略。首选的评估将采用分阶段匹配区实施,以 DTP3 覆盖率作为主要结果,并辅以零剂量、及时性、错失机会、响应性和公平性指标。数字聆听应被视为社区和服务数据中的一个哨兵输入,而不是一种具有人口代表性的措施。该途径的公共卫生价值最终取决于经过验证的担忧是否转化为可信的沟通和可达的 疫苗接种 服务,特别是对于流离失所、游牧、不安全和数字排斥的社区。

证据基础与方法
提供的证据没有明确说明审查方法、数据库、资格流程或证据基础。
共识与结论
该改编规定了负责任的参与者、离线和数字收听渠道、语言和数据系统要求、服务链接规则、冲突设置的保障措施以及评估策略。
争议与缺口
本观点认为,错误信息、信任和获取应一起审查,同时认识到索马里有关数字 疫苗 错误信息的具体证据仍然有限。
科研与实践启示
该途径的公共卫生价值最终取决于经过验证的担忧是否转化为可信的沟通和可达的 疫苗接种 服务,特别是对于流离失所、游牧、不安全和数字排斥的社区。

Closing Somalia’s childhood vaccination gap in the digital era: a perspective on misinformation, trust, and access

Authors: Abdisalam Abdullahi Mohamed
Original abstract (English)
Somalia’s childhood immunization coverage has improved, but important gaps remain across vaccine doses, populations, and places. The latest WHO/UNICEF estimates for 2025 report 77% coverage for the first dose of diphtheria-tetanus-pertussis-containing vaccine (DTP1), 72% for DTP3, and 61% for the first measles-containing vaccine (MCV1), while the 2020 Somali Health and Demographic Survey documented far lower household-survey coverage. These measures are not directly interchangeable and illustrate both progress and continuing uncertainty in a fragile data environment. This Perspective argues that misinformation, trust, and access should be examined together, while recognizing that Somalia-specific evidence on digital vaccine misinformation remains limited. Established WHO Behavioural and Social Drivers of vaccination (BeSD) and WHO/UNICEF social-listening guidance are therefore adapted into an operational pathway for Somalia rather than presented as a new behavioral theory. The adaptation specifies responsible actors, offline and digital listening channels, linguistic and data-system requirements, service-linkage rules, safeguards for conflict settings, and an evaluation strategy. A preferred evaluation would use phased matched-district implementation with DTP3 coverage as the primary outcome, complemented by zero-dose, timeliness, missed-opportunity, responsiveness, and equity indicators. Digital listening should be treated as one sentinel input among community and service data, not as a population-representative measure. The public health value of the pathway ultimately depends on whether verified concerns are translated into trusted communication and reachable vaccination services, especially for displaced, nomadic, insecure, and digitally excluded communities.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The latest WHO/UNICEF estimates for 2025 report 77% coverage for the first dose of diphtheria-tetanus-pertussis-containing vaccine (DTP1), 72% for DTP3, and 61% for the first measles-containing vaccine (MCV1), while the 2020 Somali Health and Demographic Survey documented far lower household-survey coverage.
Evidence base and method
The supplied evidence does not clearly state the review method, databases, eligibility process, or evidence base.
Consensus and conclusions
The adaptation specifies responsible actors, offline and digital listening channels, linguistic and data-system requirements, service-linkage rules, safeguards for conflict settings, and an evaluation strategy.
Controversies and gaps
This Perspective argues that misinformation, trust, and access should be examined together, while recognizing that Somalia-specific evidence on digital vaccine misinformation remains limited.
Research and practice implications
The public health value of the pathway ultimately depends on whether verified concerns are translated into trusted communication and reachable vaccination services, especially for displaced, nomadic, insecure, and digitally excluded communities.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

尽管病毒遗传多样性减少,麻疹 在全球范围内卷土重来 - 新闻-医疗

Measles resurges worldwide despite shrinking viral genetic diversity - News-Medical
新闻简报麻疹 病例在 2025 年反弹至大流行前的水平,全球报告病例为 411,913 例,而风疹 麻疹 由于序列提交有限,仍然具有挑战性。2024 年至 2025 年,欧洲区域 (EUR) 病例下降了 75%,D8 基因型是自 2018 年以来报告最频繁的 麻疹 基因型。世卫组织继续通过全球 麻疹 和风疹实验室网络 (GMRLN) 协调 麻疹。
查看新闻五要素
时间
评论:麻疹 和风疹分子 流行病学 的全球更新:2000-2025。
地点与对象
全球,重点关注欧洲区域(EUR)、美洲区域(AMR)、非洲区域(AFR)和东南亚区域(SEAR)。
事件
麻疹 病例反弹至大流行前的水平,估计报告病例仅占全球实际病例的 5% 左右。由于提交的序列有限,风疹 监测 仍然很困难。
规模、影响与风险
2025 年,全球报告了 411,913 例 麻疹 病例,与 2024 年相比,2025 年欧元下降了 75%。麻疹 遗传多样性持续下降,基因型 D8 是自 2018 年以来全球报告最频繁的。风疹 监测 的情况有限,自 2022 年以来仅记录了 59 个 RuV 序列继续。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织通过全球 麻疹 和风疹实验室网络 (GMRLN) 协调 麻疹,该网络结合流行病学报告和分子数据来追踪循环菌株和 传播。需要成员国持续参与来加强分子 监测。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles resurges worldwide despite shrinking viral genetic diversity - News-Medical

News Brief
Measles cases rebounded to pre-pandemic levels in 2025, with 411,913 reported cases globally, while rubella surveillance remains challenging due to limited sequence submissions. The European Region (EUR) saw a 75% decline in cases from 2024 to 2025, and genotype D8 has been the most frequently reported measles genotype since 2018. The WHO continues to coordinate surveillance through the Global Measles and Rubella Laboratory Network (GMRLN).
Original source text (English)
As measles cases rebound and rubella remains difficult to track, a 25-year global review examines how changing viral genetics and gaps in disease surveillance complicate efforts to eliminate both infections. Review: Global Update on Measles and Rubella Molecular Epidemiology: 2000–2025. Image Credit: Yau Ming Low / Shutterstock In a recent review of global surveillance data published in the journal Vaccines, researchers investigated the molecular epidemiology of measles and rubella (MR) viruses worldwide between 2000 and 2025, examining sequence submissions and the distribution of viral genotypes. An international team of researchers from the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), World Health Organization (WHO) headquarters and regional offices, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), and the Pasteur Institute of Tunis at University Tunis El Manar conducted the review, spanning institutions across nine countries. Measles virus (MeV) sequence submissions relative to reported cases have returned to pre-pandemic levels across all regions, but only two of the 24 historically recognized measles genotypes have been reported since 2021. The pandemic's impact on rubella surveillance remains difficult to determine, partly because few rubella virus (RuV) sequences are submitted to global surveillance databases. The review also outlines challenges facing the World Health Organization's (WHO) Global Measles and Rubella Laboratory Network (GMRLN) and stresses the need for continued member-state participation to strengthen molecular surveillance. Measles and rubella elimination remains a global public health priority. The WHO coordinates surveillance through GMRLN, which combines epidemiological reports and molecular data to track circulating strains and transmission. Member states contribute case reports and viral sequences to monitor genetic diversity, investigate outbreaks, and identify gaps in surveillance and vaccination. Global measles and rubella surveillance The review draws on global data from the Measles virus Nucleotide Surveillance (MeaNS2) and Rubella virus Nucleotide Surveillance (RubeNS2) databases through 2025. Most sequences are generated using reverse transcription-polymerase chain reaction (RT-PCR) and Sanger sequencing. Only WHO GMRLN-accredited laboratories that pass annual laboratory proficiency tests can submit data. Member states must submit representative sequences covering 80% or more of transmission chains no later than eight weeks after sample receipt. MeV molecular surveillance relies mainly on the N-450 target, a 450-nucleotide segment located near the end of the nucleocapsid (N) gene. For RuV genotyping, laboratories examine a 739-nucleotide segment of the envelope glycoprotein 1 (E1) gene, designated E-739 (739-bp window). The WHO compiles annual MR case figures using the Joint Reporting Form (JRF), developed jointly with UNICEF. MeV genotypes are classified according to sequence similarities in the hemagglutinin (H) and nucleoprotein (N) genes. Measles cases rebound after the pandemic A total of 411,913 measles cases were reported worldwide during 2025, returning to levels comparable to those before the pandemic. Reported cases are estimated to account for only around 5% of actual measles cases globally. Missed childhood vaccinations and resulting immunity gaps likely drove the resurgence. The extent to which pandemic-era declines reflected reduced transmission or disrupted surveillance remains unclear. Event guide: 10th CAR-TCR Summit eBook Navigate the latest developments in cell therapy at the 10th CAR-TCR Summit Europe. Download the latest edition Number of reported measles virus cases and sequence submissions to MeaNS2, stratified by WHO region. Reported case numbers were obtained from the WHO/UNICEF Joint Reporting Form (JRF), while sequence counts represent MeV sequences submitted by WHO GMRLN-accredited laboratories to MeaNS2. Transparency of grey bars indicates y-scale in that area of the plot and orders of magnitude between points. The European Region (EUR) recorded over 150,000 cases in 2024, its highest total since 1997, followed by a 75% decline in 2025. Cases rose from 2024 in the Region of the Americas (AMR), following previous regional measles elimination, while the African Region (AFR) and South-East Asia Region (SEAR) saw declines after their 2023 surges. Sequencing coverage was highest in the AMR, with a median annual ratio of submitted sequences to reported cases of 20% between 2000 and 2025, compared with 4% in the EUR and less than 1% in the other four WHO regions. Incomplete case reporting inflated AMR estimates, while representative outbreak sampling partly explains lower sequencing-to-case ratios in high-incidence regions. The EUR and Western Pacific Region (WPR) together contributed approximately 70% of sequences in MeaNS2. Annual distribution of MeV genotype B3 and D8 sequences submitted to MeaNS2, stratified by WHO region. Measles genetic diversity continues to decline The two remaining reported measles genotypes are B3 and D8. This reduction in genetic diversity is consistent with vaccination efforts limiting opportunities for viral transmission but makes distinguishing transmission chains through routine genotyping more difficult. D8 has been the most frequently reported genotype globally since 2018, except in 2021, and accounted for nearly all genetically characterized cases during the AMR resurgence in 2025. B3 was more common in the Eastern Mediterranean Region (EMR), while both genotypes were reported at comparable frequencies in the AFR and WPR. Europe also reported more B3 than D8 sequences in 2025 for the first time since 2021. Sequencing beyond the N-450 target has also increased since 2022, potentially improving transmission tracking. Among named strains, which represent frequently reported N-450 sequences, MVs/Gir Somnath.IND/42.16[D8] had the highest cumulative case count, with 6,595 recorded cases across 72 countries over a five-year period beginning in 2016. Rubella surveillance reveals major data gaps RuV molecular surveillance remains considerably more limited, with approximately one-tenth as many sequence submissions as measles. A shorter detection window, fewer detected cases, fewer accredited sequencing laboratories, lower viral RNA concentrations in clinical specimens, and the viral genome's high guanine-cytosine content may explain this gap. Rubella trends also varied by region. Reported cases in the AFR rose more than 20-fold, from 865 in 2000 to 19,669 in 2025, amid outbreaks in Nigeria, South Africa, and Uganda. The WPR peaked at 126,827 cases in 2008, before declining during 2020–2025. Some historical increases in reported cases may partly reflect improved surveillance sensitivity. Meanwhile, cases in the EUR and AMR fell approximately 1,000-fold since 2000. During the decade through 2025, approximately 247,000 rubella cases were reported globally, compared with more than 3.5 million measles cases. Incomplete reporting also means that the true rubella burden is likely higher. Fewer than one in 1,000 reported rubella cases were sequenced in previous years. Only 59 RuV sequences belonging to genotypes 1G and 2B were documented from 2022 onward, including just six in 2025. Genotype 2B has been the only genotype reported globally since 2024, while 1G, previously predominant in the AFR, was last reported in 2023. Genotype 1E predominated in the WPR during expanded surveillance between 2018 and 2020 but has not been reported since 2021. Limited sequencing coverage prevents definitive conclusions about whether other rubella genotypes have disappeared. Improving genomic surveillance and vaccination coverage “Sequence data alone cannot reliably reconstruct transmission patterns, particularly for measles.” Sustaining MR elimination will require investment in laboratory capacity, standardized sequencing protocols, quality assurance, surveillance, and vaccination. Whole-genome sequencing (WGS) and other extended sequencing approaches could improve the resolution of measles transmission tracking beyond the commonly used N-450 region. Their wider use is limited by costs, technical expertise, and inconsistent sequencing quality. Complete coding sequence (CDS) analysis could offer useful genetic detail while avoiding some technical challenges associated with non-coding regions, but its practical implementation would require high-throughput sequencing technology. Mutations identified through extended sequencing require interpretation alongside epidemiological data, case timing, and rates of genetic change in the virus, since longer genomic regions are naturally expected to contain more mutations. Named strains do not necessarily represent individual transmission chains. Differences in surveillance sensitivity, laboratory capacity, reporting completeness, and the proportion of laboratory-confirmed cases limit direct regional comparisons. Molecular surveillance must complement epidemiological investigations, with closer collaboration between laboratory scientists and epidemiologists needed to guide immunization efforts. No measles vaccine-escape strains have been reported. Maintaining high vaccination coverage and sensitive case detection remains central to MR elimination. Source: Journal reference: - Weston, T., Cheng, C., Warrener, L. et al. (2026). Global Update on Measles and Rubella Molecular Epidemiology: 2000–2025. Vaccines, 14(11), 964. DOI: 10.3390/vaccines14110964, https://www.mdpi.com/2076-393X/14/11/964

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles outbreaks raise serious health concerns for expectant mothers and newborns - News-Medical

News Brief
A recent CDC report, published in the New England Journal of Medicine Evidence, highlights the growing health concerns associated with measles outbreaks in the United States, particularly for expectant mothers and newborns. The report notes that 112 cases of measles were reported in pregnant women and 13 cases of congenital measles in babies during 2025 and 2026. These outbreaks have raised alarms due to the increased risks faced by pregnant women, including non-labor-related hospitalizations, pneumonia, and death, as well as potential serious birth outcomes such as stillbirths, miscarriages, and premature births. Newborns with congenital measles are at risk for severe illness, encephalitis, and subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, a rare and fatal neurological disorder. The report also emphasizes the importance of measles vaccination for women of child-bearing age and passive immunization for newborns exposed to the virus in utero. However, the federal government's response has been criticized for undermining public confidence in vaccines, which may have contributed to the nation's loss of measles elimination status. Clinicians are advised to focus on vaccination of those around pregnant women to reduce their risk of exposure.
Original source text (English)
As measles infections increasingly appear in U.S. adults-a new CDC report is raising concerns about a heightened risk of congenital measles and infections in pregnant women. For expectant mothers, evidence suggests that infections can increase the risk of non-labor related hospitalizations, pneumonia, and death. Congenital measles can occur when a pregnant woman becomes infected with measles during pregnancy and passes the virus to the fetus in the womb. Infants can face severe illness, encephalitis, and subacute sclerosing panencephalitis-a rare, progressive, and often fatal neurological disorder. The new report, published in the New England Journal of Medicine Evidence, connects large measles outbreaks across the U.S. in 2025 and 2026 to 112 cases of measles in pregnant women and 13 cases of congenital measles in babies. In a November 25 episode of Public Health On Call, William Moss, MD, MPH, professor in Epidemiology, unpacks the new government analysis, including the measles threat to expectant mothers and newborns, and what it means in the context of ongoing outbreaks and the country's lapsed measles elimination status. Topics discussed: - The status of measles in the U.S., including current outbreaks in Pennsylvania and the nation's recent loss of elimination status - The new report from the CDC on congenital measles and why increases in adult measles infections are creating more openings for congenital measles - The possible health risks facing pregnant women who are infected with measles, and their newborns - How clinicians should approach measles vaccination and treatment for women of child-bearing age, pregnant women, and babies - The federal response to measles and congenital measles since 2025 Key quotes from the episode On the current status of measles and congenital measles in the U.S.: "Tragically we now have over 6,000 measles cases reported in the United States just this year." (01:54) "Earlier this month, on November 17, the Pan American Health Organization announced that the United States had lost its measles elimination status. Measles elimination is defined as no continuous transmission of the same measles virus strain for at least 12 months." (02:30) "A report from the Center for Disease Control and Prevention published in the New England Journal of Medicine Evidence ... report[ed] an astonishing 112 cases of measles in pregnant women and 13 cases of congenital measles." (03:10) "This is the largest case series that I'm aware of, of both measles in pregnancy and of congenital measles." (03:44) "According to the Center for Disease Control and Prevention, about 41% of measles cases in the United States have been in adults. If we look at Pennsylvania, for example, more than two-thirds of measles cases have been in adults 18 years or older." (04:18) On the health risks facing pregnant women who contract measles and their fetuses: "In the CDC report, almost a quarter of the women required hospitalization for non-labor related causes. Eleven of the 112, so about 10% of women, developed pneumonia. ... they're also at risk for serious birth outcomes: there can be stillbirths or miscarriages, premature births." (06:22) "During the last really big outbreak of measles here in the United States between 1989 and 1991 ... Pregnant women [with measles infections] were twice as likely to be admitted to the hospital, three times more likely to be diagnosed with pneumonia, and six times more likely to die of measles." (07:19) "We do know that infants ... with congenital measles can have some of the similar signs and symptoms of measles that we see in older children and adults: fever, rash." (8:10) "[Newborns are] also at risk for encephalitis, and there's even some suggestion ... that these infants may be at greater risk for a rare neurologic outcome called subacute sclerosing panencephalitis ... It's a severe, fatally progressive neurologic disease with a kind of defective measles virus in the brain." (08:27) On measles vaccination and treatment for pregnant women and infants: "Before pregnancy, make sure that you are vaccinated so that you are protected and can protect your infant." (15:37) "The measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine is not indicated during pregnancy, and that's in part because of that immune-suppressed state of pregnant women and the fact that these are attenuated viral vaccines, so they need to replicate in order to induce an immune response. So, we can't vaccinate women during pregnancy." (09:54) "Instead of what we call active immunization-giving a vaccine and inducing that person's immune system to be trained to fight that pathogen-we give what's called passive immunization. And so, it's something called intravenous immunoglobulin." (10:30) "Immunoglobulin is also recommended at birth for newborns who have been exposed to measles virus in utero, if the mom has measles late in the third trimester or prior to birth, and that too can reduce the morbidity associated with congenital measles." (11:10) "During pregnancy, what women need to do is really ensure that the people around them, their family members, their friends who they're coming into contact with, are vaccinated against measles to really reduce their risk of exposure." (16:01) On the response to measles from federal health officials: "We've not seen the strong response from the federal government that is needed promoting measles vaccination, and, in fact, we've seen the opposite." (14:30) "We've seen messaging that undermines public confidence in vaccines in general and the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine, specifically." (14:40) "I am sure that [the response from federal officials] has ... played a role in our loss of measles elimination status here in the United States and these continued large outbreaks. It's not the only explanation ... but the federal response has not helped and almost certainly set us back." (14:49)

B routine priorityPublic-health news

‘Even Washing My Hands Hurt’: This Is What It's Like To Get Measles

News Brief
A 26-year-old adult in Pennsylvania, who had received MMR vaccines as a child, contracted measles and experienced severe symptoms including a painful rash, fever, and cough. This case highlights the importance of measles vaccination, especially for adults, as the disease is highly contagious and can lead to serious complications like immune amnesia and encephalitis. Over 315 people have been hospitalized, and eight have died from measles in Pennsylvania.
Original source text (English)
LOADING ERROR LOADING The first rash didn’t seem to worry Mike Z.’s doctor. Mike, then 26, had spent days in and out of a hospital waiting room with a severe sinus infection when he pointed out a rash he noticed spreading across his body. The doctor called it an allergic reaction and prescribed penicillin. The next morning, Mike noticed another rash across his torso, and this one felt nothing like the first. “Since it was so sudden, I went to the ER,” Mike, who asked to withhold his last name to talk about his illness, told HuffPost. It was then that Mike was diagnosed with measles, despite having had his MMR vaccines as a child. Mike had a strong fever and a deep cough, but the worst part was the rash, which had spread from his core to his whole body. “My entire body was hurting and itchy,” he said. “Taking a shower was painful; even washing my hands hurt because the rash got painful when exposed to heat.” Mike described the rash as so painful and itchy that he couldn’t sleep. “I tried every nonprescription cream I could find, but nothing helped,” he said. “A lot of them were actively making my life worse from the extra scratching — my skin had micro-scratches that burned.” The worst of the pain lasted two to three weeks, he remembers. Mike’s case is the exception for MMR vaccines. Two doses are about 97% effective, and breakthrough cases are uncommon. Pennsylvania, for example, has a recorded 1,561 confirmed measles cases as of reporting, and less than 1% of them were vaccinated. It doesn’t mean the vaccine failed or doesn’t work. Dr. Paul Offit, director of the Vaccine Education Center at Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia, compared it to wearing a seatbelt. “We put on seatbelts, which dramatically lowers the risk that we could die in a car accident,” he said. “It doesn’t eliminate it.” For many adults, measles is unfamiliar territory. Mike grew up after the United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 and never saw anyone with it. Many of today’s adults have never seen what measles looks or feels like; many dismiss it as “ just a rash. ” “When we eliminated measles in the year 2000, we eliminated the memory of measles,” Offit said. “People don’t remember how sick or dead this virus can make you.” On Nov. 17, the U.S. officially lost its measles elimination status . Out of the cases in Pennsylvania, over 315 people are hospitalized, and eight have died from measles. Many of the patients are adults. “We seem to be focusing on making sure children are immunized. ... Adults who’ve never been naturally infected ... and, for whatever reason, weren’t vaccinated are now at high risk. And they are more likely to develop severe disease,” Offit said. Many younger doctors haven’t seen measles either, and cases are often missed at first since the rash shows up about a week into contracting the disease. It started like the flu, Wesley D., 32, told HuffPost. Wesley, who asked to remain anonymous to talk about his condition, said he received two MMR doses as a child, as his school required. He described a sudden fever, a persistent dry cough and not being able to stomach any food. “Every morning I would wake up and feel a little better, but once I had been awake a few hours in the afternoons, I started to feel worse again,” he said. “Sleep was incredibly unpleasant. I felt like I was never truly deeply asleep — I was tossing and turning; my muscles would have that kind of faint ache.” About a week in, Wesley saw blotches on his face and panicked. His doctor diagnosed him with measles, but Wesley’s first blood test came back negative. His doctor believed it had been drawn too early, and early testing can miss an active measles infection, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention . The doctor also told Wesley he had likely been contagious the week prior when he thought he just had the flu. The CDC reports measles will spread to other people from four days before the rash appears to four days after. If someone has measles, up to 90% of the people around them who are not immune will also be infected, the CDC states. “I am somebody who will shake off a cold or a flu or a fever, and carry on as normal,” Wesley said. “But measles was really unpleasant. Sickest I’ve ever felt, and when it goes on for as long as it did, it really does start to almost make one angry and frustrated. It just goes on and on and on.” Natalya Maisheva via Getty Images The conversation around measles tends to focus on children, but adults can get extremely sick as well. Why Losing Elimination Status Is So Worrying ― Even If You’re Vaccinated Not everyone who is vaccinated has the same protection. People born after 1989 got two doses, which Offit said protects roughly 97-98%. Those born between 1968 and 1989 likely got one dose, which is about 93-94%. Dr. Gregory Poland, director of research at the Atria Research and Global Health Institute, said adults vaccinated in the 1960s may have received a vaccine that didn’t protect them. If you have a record of two doses of the MMR vaccine after your first birthday, you are considered protected and don’t need another dose, HuffPost previously reported . You can check with your parents or your state’s immunization registry to find your vaccine history. If you were born before 1957, you are also considered immune. If you cannot find your vaccine records , or if you received the vaccine between 1963 and 1967, it’s recommended you get a booster shot (unless you are pregnant or have severe immune deficiencies; then check with your doctor first). There is no harm in getting another dose of the MMR vaccine if you are already immune, according to the CDC . You can also get a blood test, known as the titer test , to check for measles antibodies — a positive result indicates immunity. Poland said measles is the most contagious disease known to humans. He compared it to the flu: If you caught the flu, you might spread it to a couple of people. With measles, you would spread it to 12 to 16 people, and they would spread it to another 12 to 16 people each. “It becomes exponential; I think people don’t realize that,” Poland said. “They don’t realize that it isn’t just a childhood little fever and rash. It can be a deadly disease, a morbid disease when you look at the complications.” Vaccinated people who get measles typically have milder illness, but the disease itself can be serious. “About one per thousand people will develop encephalitis,” Poland said, referring to the brain swelling. “About 10 per 100,000, so it’s pretty rare, will develop SSPE, subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, which you will die from. There is no treatment.” Overall, among people who get measles, around 20% will end up in the hospital and 80% or so will develop immune amnesia, Poland said. Immune amnesia occurs when the virus wipes out a body’s immune memory cells and makes it harder to fight off other germs. Mike believes he experienced immune amnesia after contracting measles in 2024. “I was constantly sick for a year,” Mike said. “Seasonal flu hits much harder now.” Vaccination isn’t a force field. How protected you are also depends on who’s around you, Offit pointed out. “If you’re living in a highly unvaccinated community, you have a greater chance of contracting the virus as compared to if you live in a highly vaccinated community.” The stakes extend past personal preferences. Immunocompromised people can’t receive the vaccine, Poland said, so “they are completely dependent on the immunity status of those around them in school, work, church, community, whatever it might be.” “It didn’t change my mind on vaccines,” Mike said about his experience. “It made me more worried about the lack of vaccines. You wish more [people] were vaccinated, so I could have been spared.” | Submit a tip

Over 315 people are hospitalized, and eight have died from measles in Pennsylvania. The disease is highly contagious, with up to 90% of unvaccinated people around an infected individual becoming infected. Immune amnesia and encephalitis are potential complications.

The article discusses the need for adults to check their vaccination status and get booster shots if necessary. The CDC recommends checking vaccine records or getting a blood test for immunity.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

What the Return of Measles Means for the U.S. - Time Magazine

News Brief
Health Matters Weekdays · The latest health news and expert advice on living well. Thank you for signing up! 17, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) declared that measles is no longer considered "eliminated" in the U.S. In the past year, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recorded more than 5,800 cases of measles—the highest caseload since 1991. Vaccination programs and increased monitoring of incoming measles cases helped the U.S. to eliminate measles in 2000, meaning there was no consistent spread of the virus within the country for more than a year.
Original source text (English)
Health Matters Weekdays · The latest health news and expert advice on living well. Thank you for signing up! On Nov. 17, the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) declared that measles is no longer considered "eliminated" in the U.S. In the past year, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recorded more than 5,800 cases of measles—the highest caseload since 1991. Vaccination programs and increased monitoring of incoming measles cases helped the U.S. to eliminate measles in 2000, meaning there was no consistent spread of the virus within the country for more than a year. Since then, sporadic cases of the virus have been reported—but from people who had traveled to other parts of the world where the disease is endemic. No longer. So far, the CDC reports that 47 states have reported at least one case of measles, and wastewater reports of cases of the virus have increased in recent months. “What makes this different from pop-up cases that we expect year to year is that we failed to interrupt the chain of transmission of the virus,” says Steph Batalis, research fellow at Georgetown University’s Center for Security and Emerging Technology. “That’s a big deal. We have tools to do that, yet we failed to do so. That should raise serious questions about the conditions that allowed that to happen.” Days after the announcement, Secretary of Health and Human Services (HHS) Robert F. Kennedy Jr. posted on X that HHS will "work to regain U.S. measles elimination status. I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles.” While Kennedy went on to blame COVID for interrupting childhood vaccinations, public-health officials point to several other more recent actions that Kennedy, a long-time skeptic of vaccines, has taken that may have also contributed to the rise of measles cases in the U.S. In 2025, Kennedy dismissed the CDC’s Advisory Committee for Immunization Practices, an independent group of experts that sets vaccine recommendations for the U.S. that state health departments and professional organizations generally follow, and replaced the committee with members who shared his vaccine skepticism. HHS also attempted to revise the childhood immunization schedule, removing recommendations for most children to receive annual flu and COVID shots, and emphasizing shared-decision making about vaccines between families and physicians. Professional health organizations—including the American Academy of Pediatrics, the American College of Physicians, and the Infectious Diseases Society of America—sued HHS over these changes. A court suspended the new ACIP from continuing to meet and reverted the childhood immunization schedule back to its original recommendations. Such confusion around vaccines has likely played a significant role in the U.S. measles outbreaks of the past year, says Dr. Jon Andrus, professor of global health at the Milken Institute School of Public Health at George Washington University. “Any successful public-health program requires excellence in scientific and operational strategies about how you are vaccinating, when you vaccinate them, and what vaccines you use, all based on evidence,” he says. “And none of that works without political commitment. If that is undermined, and if the messages coming from the highest level sows seeds of doubt, then you have people doubting the strategies and not vaccinating their child.” He and other public-health experts say that the loss of the measles elimination status isn’t merely a symbolic change. It’s a warning sign of a weakened public-health system fueled by deteriorating trust in scientific experts and mixed messages from health leaders who the public normally turn to for advice. And the change in the U.S.’s status may have deeper consequences for public health, beyond just more cases of measles. Recommended Stories Here's what's worrying experts. There are long-term consequences of getting measles Some of the public’s attitudes about vaccines—including the one for measles, which is part of the MMR series—center around unsubstantiated claims that the vaccines contain toxic chemicals that do more harm than good, and that the measles shot in particular is linked to a higher risk of autism, a claim that has for years been scientifically debunked. Because measles has not been common in the U.S. for decades, a generation of parents and doctors have not seen how devastating the disease can be. “Measles in Latin means ‘miserable,’” says Andrus. Having measles can cause pneumonia, ear infections, and encephalitis, or brain swelling. But even if a child recovers from a measles infection, the disease may affect his or her health for years to come. “Measles infections can contribute to deafness, blindness, and, depending on the neurological insult, intellectual and physical disability as well,” says Dr. Micah Niermann, an internal medicine and pediatric physician at Gillette Children’s in St. Paul, Minn. “Some may need lifelong care, and their lives and the lives of their families are altered permanently.” More recently, scientists have also uncovered another way the virus can impact a person’s health, even if they recover from the infection. “The measles virus can attack immune cells and make them less effective for some prolonged period of time,” says Dr. Andrew Racine, president of the American Academy of Pediatrics. “We don’t know how long, but for that period after measles, the child is more susceptible to other kinds of infections that they are ordinarily immune to.” The measles virus has the ability to wipe out whatever memory the immune cells have accumulated to recognize other pathogens—including those introduced by vaccines. “A child who has had measles is at risk of suffering from or dying from other vaccine-preventable diseases, and that risk goes out three years or longer,” says Andrus. There is also a rare but still meaningful risk of longer term consequences of measles known as subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a condition caused by the measles virus destroying brain cells. SSPE’s symptoms can start years after a measles infection but cannot be triggered by the measles vaccine. Children may start to show changes in personality and experience seizures or involuntary movements of their arms and legs, which then leads to dementia and problems eating, swallowing, and breathing. The condition is almost always fatal. Children under two who get measles are at higher risk of developing SPPE later in life, and the best way to prevent the condition is to protect those children from getting measles in the first place with the vaccine, or for those too young to be vaccinated, to ensure those around them are vaccinated so they are less likely to get exposed to measles. Some people may need a booster shot Most adults who have received two doses of the MMR vaccine do not need to get a booster; the two doses provide 97% protection against infection for a lifetime, according to the CDC. Most people who were born before 1957 also don’t need an additional shot since they were likely exposed to measles because there was no vaccine yet available. The only groups that may need to consider a booster are people who might not know if they received two doses of the vaccine or people who are immunocompromised, such as those undergoing chemotherapy. They might benefit from the booster after, but not while, they are undergoing chemotherapy, to give their immune systems additional support. “Say you are undergoing a bone marrow transplant or chemotherapy: this adds another element of risk assessment for patients,” says Niermann. “There are people at risk who could get ill from measles.” Another group of people who should consider a booster are those born from 1963 to 1967. In that time, an old version of the vaccine was used that might not be as effective as the current version, which relies on a weakened, live version of the measles virus. People who are currently 59-63 should discuss with their health care provider whether they need a booster. Outbreaks won't stop at measles “Measles is the canary in the coal mine,” says Racine. “This is not just about measles. The reason we’re seeing measles first is that it’s more contagious than any other infectious disease." If one person is infected, for instance, 90% of people in close contact with that person can get infected as well. And the virus can stay airborne from an infected person’s cough or sneeze for up to two hours. "It’s as if measles is knocking on the door of every household in the country saying, ‘Guess what, I’m here. I’m back,'" Racine says. "'You may have thought I got lost and went someplace else. I’m here to tell you that you forgot to lock the door, and I’m coming in. And not only that, but I’m not by myself. Take a look behind me, I’ve brought a bunch of friends: influenza, hepatitis B, Haemophilus influenzae, pertussis, whooping cough. Look down the list, and you see polio back there too. They’re coming—we’re all coming.’” Measles is so contagious that it’s often the first outbreak public-health experts see as childhood vaccination rates decline. “We often say that measles outbreaks are a signal of a weaker immunization system,” says Dr. William Moss, professor at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health. “This is a red flag and tells us we’re not doing what we need to be doing to control measles.” As vaccination rates fall in general, other vaccine-preventable diseases will start to spike. “I will be surprised if we don’t start to see more cases of congenital rubella and mumps,” says Dr. Wendy Armstrong, president of the Infectious Diseases Society of America and head of the division of infectious diseases at the University of Colorado Anschutz School of Medicine. Measles outbreaks could threaten U.S. biosecurity The measles outbreak may also affect the U.S.’s ability to protect itself from infectious threats. The health care costs associated with measles—in terms of immediate care and the potential longer terms consequences if they develop SPPE—are already burdening overstretched local and state health resources. Doctors and public-health experts who could be dedicated to focusing on other threats are now required to manage preventable illnesses. “Public-health departments are on the front lines, and if they are addressing measles outbreaks, which is something that is avoidable, then there are opportunity costs for how they are able to address other issues,” says Niermann. Even more concerning, says Batalis, are the mixed messages coming from federal health authorities. “If people are looking to get [health] information for themselves, the question becomes: Who is filling that vacuum of information?” she says. “If it’s not filled by trustworthy sources and if the government is discrediting trustworthy sources, then who fills the gap? Now we’re seeing reports that a lot of vaccine disinformation is coming from bots, which raises a lot of questions about how that makes us more vulnerable, and how could adversaries exploit that. There is a security consideration in addition to public health.” How can the U.S. regain its status as having eliminated measles? “The very first thing that needs to happen is a recognition that an error has been made,” says Racine. “And unfortunately, in the condition that we’re currently in, the folks who are in charge of this have not been willing or able to recognize that a mistake has been made here. Until that happens, we are not able to have the full force of all the government authorities—from local, state, and national levels—working in conjunction with pediatricians and the community and public-health officials to seriously address what is going on.” While CDC director Erica Schwartz has said that the CDC is deploying resources to areas experiencing measles outbreaks, Racine says those cases will continue to spread if there isn’t a stronger and more unified message about the best way to control measles: by supporting vaccination and reiterating that it is safe. While both Kennedy and Schwartz have said they support measles vaccines, Kennedy has also stated that protection from the vaccine “waned quickly” and that the shot has not been “safely tested”—positions not supported by scientific evidence. “There are going to be long-term consequence of what is happening today,” says Racine

Login Get App News Hospitals Finance Diagnostics People Movement M&A Pharma Policy Slideshows Research Medical Devices Industry View all News Exclusives Leaders Speak Events Awards Webinars Brand Solutions More Newsletters MasterClasses About Us Contact Us Advertise With Us Search Pharma Policy Medical Devices Diagnostics Industry Hospitals Podcast Integrated Campaigns Bespoke Events Advertorial Virtual Engagement Videos Digital Branding & Awareness More More Industry 1 min read Global measles deaths fall to historic low in 2025, WHO says The UN health agency estimated 9.8 million measles cases and about 72,000 deaths worldwide in 2025 - down 74% from 37.8 million cases in 2000, with deaths falling roughly 91% from about 769,000. Reuters Published On Nov 28, 2026 at 07:23 AM IST Set ETHealthworld as your preferred source on Google Copy Link Share on WhatsApp Share on Linkedin Share on X Share on Telegram Share on Facebook Global measles deaths hit a historic low in 2025, the World Health Organization said on Friday, although millions of children still missed vaccinations and outbreaks persisted in dozens of countries. The UN health agency estimated 9.8 million measles cases and about 72,000 deaths worldwide in 2025 - down 74% from 37.8 million cases in 2000, with deaths falling roughly 91% from about 769,000. WHO said global measles immunization rates remained largely unchanged in 2025, but cautioned the trend hides declines in some regions, including the Americas, where coverage has dropped from levels above 90% before the COVID-19 pandemic. Advt Several countries have recently lost their measles elimination status, showing that without high vaccination coverage, surveillance and political will, the virus can "quickly regain a foothold," WHO said. Twenty-nine million children were unvaccinated or under-vaccinated against measles in 2025, the agency said on a press call. WHO estimated that 20.8 million infants missed a first dose of vaccine and another 8.3 million missed a second dose. "The recent setbacks we've seen are not a failure of the measles vaccine, they're a failure to reach every child with the vaccine," said Dr. Kate O'Brien, director of WHO's Department of Immunization, Vaccines and Biologicals. WHO said 59 countries reported large or disruptive outbreaks in 2025, compared with 61 in 2024 and 58 in 2023. CALL FOR INVESTMENT O'Brien called for countries to "double down on the investments in their programs," to ensure that the vaccine is available at times and places that people can easily access. WHO warned that immunization and surveillance programmes face increasing financial pressures even as the costs of closing immunity gaps and responding to outbreaks rise. O'Brien said the measles vaccine delivers one of the highest returns on investment of any vaccine, calling it "a best buy." 专家敦促 疫苗接种,因为 麻疹 暴发 威胁未受保护的社区 - 新闻-医疗

Experts urge vaccination as measles outbreaks threaten unprotected communities - News-Medical
新闻简报Measles is one of the most contagious viruses known to humans-and outbreaks are increasingly more common in the U.S. Ensuring your family is up to date on routine immunizations, including the measles vaccine (often given as the combined measles, mumps, and rubella or MMR vaccine), is the most effective safeguard kids and adults have against severe infection and even death. Widespread use of measles vaccines over the past 50 years has helped prevent more than 93 million deaths worldwide. Staying safe when measles transmission is high Quick facts about measles: The virus can spread through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes, or talks. Respiratory droplets can survive for up to two hours in the air and on surfaces. Measles disproportionately affects infants, children, and young adults up to age 19. More than 90% of all infections are in people who have not yet been immunized against measles.

Original source text (English)
Measles is one of the most contagious viruses known to humans-and outbreaks are increasingly more common in the U.S. today. Ensuring your family is up to date on routine immunizations, including the measles vaccine (often given as the combined measles, mumps, and rubella or MMR vaccine), is the most effective safeguard kids and adults have against severe infection and even death. Widespread use of measles vaccines over the past 50 years has helped prevent more than 93 million deaths worldwide. Staying safe when measles transmission is high Quick facts about measles: The virus can spread through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes, or talks. Respiratory droplets can survive for up to two hours in the air and on surfaces. Measles disproportionately affects infants, children, and young adults up to age 19. More than 90% of all infections are in people who have not yet been immunized against measles. Two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% protection against measles. Michael Smit, MD, MSPH, Medical Director of Infection Prevention and Control in the Division of Infectious Diseases at Children's Hospital Los Angeles, has spent his career exploring how infections spread and how outbreaks can be prevented. "There is ongoing potential for worsening widespread outbreaks, especially if contagious individuals with measles enter communities with low vaccination rates," says Dr. Smit. "This risk is highlighted with the loss of measles-elimination status by the United States." "If your goal is to avoid catching measles and spreading it to others in your community," he adds, "getting the vaccine is the most effective choice you can make." Identifying a measles infection Measles is a potentially serious infection most often recognized by the rash that accompanies its flu-like symptoms, which include: Fever Cough Runny nose Red, watery eyes Red rash 3-4 days after symptoms begin, which may start at the face and spread downward If you think your child might have measles: Contact your pediatrician right away to discuss next steps. Isolate your child in a separate room with a closed door, and do not send them to school or daycare. Try calling the emergency room or doctor's office before going-measles is highly contagious and requires special infection prevention precautions. Use CHLA's Symptom Checker if you're unsure and need help deciding what level of care your child may need. How measles spreads Measles is passed from person to person through respiratory droplets that leave a person's body when they breathe, talk, cough, or sneeze. These droplets can linger in the air or on surfaces for up to two hours, which is one of the reasons it's so contagious. You can come into contact with these droplets by: Breathing in droplets in the air Touching the infected person Touching an item the infected person's respiratory droplets have landed on What is the risk of measles infection for vaccinated people? If your child is up to date on their routine immunizations, their risk of infection is low-even if they come into contact with an infected person. The measles vaccine is highly protective, activating your body's natural defenses against the virus. Still, it's important to communicate with your doctor and continue to monitor for symptoms and potential complications if your child has been exposed to someone with the infection. What do I do if someone in my family was exposed to measles? They may want to take additional steps to protect your child from measles after being exposed. It may involve giving them a dose of antibodies against measles, but must be done shortly after exposure and before onset of symptoms. Measles infection, diagnosis, and treatment Is a measles infection that bad? While most children recover from a measles infection, they can become seriously ill for a week or longer and may even require hospitalization. About 1 in 5 unvaccinated people with measles are hospitalized. Potential serious complications from measles include: Pneumonia Encephalitis ( inflammation of the brain) which can lead to brain damage and hearing loss Subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare but serious condition that can appear years after infection Death "Measles is not a benign infection, like a common cold, where you get it and there are typically no permanent risks," explains Dr. Smit, noting that 1 to 3 in every 1,000 children who contract measles die. "Sometimes, these complications can occur years after an infection." How is measles diagnosed and treated? Diagnosis: Your doctor may run blood, throat, and urine tests to confirm the diagnosis. Treatment: Treatment for measles is largely supportive care. You will likely be instructed to drink fluids, rest, and stay home from school or work. Monitoring: Your doctor will likely recommend you stay in close contact for several weeks to monitor for complications. Frequently asked questions about the measles vaccine When should children get the measles vaccine? Children should receive two doses of the vaccine; the first at 12–15 months of age and the second around ages 4–6. How quickly does the measles vaccine work? You will generally develop full protection against measles about a month after the vaccine is given. However, receiving the vaccine after exposure but before the onset of measles can still help reduce illness severity. How effective is the measles vaccine? The measles vaccine provides long-lasting protection. Two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% protection against measles. One dose provides 93% protection against infection. How do I know whether my child has had the measles vaccine? If you're unable to access vaccine records for your child or yourself, check with your child's pediatrician to see if getting another measles vaccine is recommended. California residents can also look up their own vaccine records on the California Department of Public Health website. How do I protect my child if they're unable to be vaccinated? The best way to protect a baby or immunocompromised child is to ensure that all family members, relatives, and household guests are fully immunized against measles (and other illnesses, including pertussis). Follow public health warnings in your area (for Californians, refer to the California Department of Public Health) for updates. Can adults get the measles vaccine? Yes. Adults who have never received the vaccine are encouraged to be immunized. Medical staff will administer the two vaccine doses at least 28 days apart. Check your medical records for proof of vaccination. You may also be able to have a blood test that checks for antibodies if you're unsure if you've received a measles vaccine. Ask your doctor about this. Is the measles vaccine expensive? The cost of the vaccine is covered by most health insurance plans, and by programs such as Vaccines for Children. L.A. County residents can check with the Los Angeles County Department of Public Health for more information. We are traveling before my baby is old enough for the measles vaccine. What should I do? Unless travel is necessary, it's best to postpone travel until children have been fully immunized-but infants can receive a measles vaccine as young as 6 months of age if traveling to an area experiencing a measles outbreak or ongoing transmission. Important note: This early vaccination does not count as a routine immunization because it may not be as effective as when the child is 1 year of age or older. Your child will still need the two routine immunizations against measles once they're old enough

卡尔霍恩县确诊 2 例 麻疹 病例,为 2013 年以来首次

2 measles cases confirmed in Calhoun County, first since 2013
新闻简报2026 年 11 月 24 日,卡尔霍恩县公共卫生部门确认居民中出现两例 麻疹 病例,这是 2013 年以来的首例。两例病例均涉及曾在国内旅行过的未接种疫苗的成年人。一名患者被送往医院,这凸显了出现肺炎、脑肿胀甚至死亡等严重并发症的可能性。卫生官员正在调查这些病例,确定潜在的接触者,并与奥克劳恩医院合作通知那些可能接触过的人。建议暴露者监测 21 天的症状,并在可能的情况下在 72 小时内寻求 疫苗接种。该部门建议在 12 月 6 日之前待在家里并避免前往公共场所,以防止进一步传播。麻疹病毒 可以在空气中存活长达两个小时,未接种疫苗的个体风险更高。应对措施包括接触者追踪、预防措施和公众意识工作。

卡尔霍恩县公共卫生部门于 2026 年 11 月 24 日确认了两例 麻疹 病例。事件日期没有明确说明,但这些病例被描述为 2013 年以来的首例。麻疹 的潜伏期为 7 至 21 天,建议暴露者留在家中直到 12 月 6 日。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

2 measles cases confirmed in Calhoun County, first since 2013

News Brief
On November 24, 2026, the Calhoun County Public Health Department confirmed two measles cases in residents, marking the first since 2013. Both cases involved unvaccinated adults who had traveled domestically. One individual was hospitalized, highlighting the potential for serious complications such as pneumonia, brain swelling, or even death. Health officials are investigating the cases, identifying potential exposures, and working with Oaklawn Hospital to notify those who may have been in contact. Exposed individuals are advised to monitor for symptoms for 21 days and seek vaccination within 72 hours if possible. The department recommends staying home and avoiding public settings until December 6 to prevent further spread. The measles virus can live in the air for up to two hours, and unvaccinated individuals are at higher risk. The response includes contact tracing, prevention measures, and public awareness efforts.

Jackson 2 measles cases confirmed in Calhoun County, first since 2013 Updated: Nov. 24, 2026, 6:00 p.m. |Published: Nov. 24, 2026, 5:48 p.m. A combination measles, mumps and rubella vaccine stand in a tray at a Pennsylvania Department of Health measles vaccination clinic located in Lancaster County in Peach Bottom, Pa., Tuesday, Oct. 20, 2026. (AP Photo/Matt Rourke)AP Photo/Matt Rourke Set as Google Preferred Source By Ryan Boldrey | rboldrey@mlive.com CALHOUN COUNTY, MI — The Calhoun County Public Health Department has confirmed two cases of measles in Calhoun County residents and is working closely with the Michigan Department of Health and Human Services to investigate the cases, identify potential exposures and help prevent further spread of the virus. Both cases are adults who travelled domestically and were not vaccinated against measles, according to officials. One of the individuals has been hospitalized. These are the first confirmed cases of measles reported in Calhoun County since 2013, when a resident was exposed to measles after traveling internationally. The two confirmed cases highlight how quickly measles can spread among individuals who are not immune to the virus. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in the air. Health officials warn that anyone who was present at the Oaklawn Hospital Emergency Department waiting room, 200 N. Madison St. in Marshall, between 10:30 p.m. Sunday, Nov. 22 and 12:30 a.m. may have been exposed. While measles was once considered eliminated in the United States, outbreaks continue to occur as vaccination rates decline and travel increases opportunities for exposure. “Measles is more than ‘just a rash’ and can include serious complications such as pneumonia, brain swelling or even death,” said Dr. William Nettleton, medical director of the Calhoun County Public Health Department. “Thankfully measles can be prevented with the MMR vaccine.” Nettleton suggests people check with their doctor or examine their immunization records to ensure they are protected against measles and to get vaccinated if not. What to do if you were exposed Anyone who was present at the Oaklawn Hospital Emergency Department waiting room late Sunday night should monitor themselves for symptoms of measles for 21 days as they may have been exposed. If symptoms appear, people are asked not to seek medical treatment in person without calling the doctor’s office, urgent care or emergency room first. MMR vaccine can prevent measles infection if administered within 72 hours of exposure. Therefore, exposed individuals have until Wednesday night, Nov. 25, to seek vaccination. For those who cannot receive the MMR vaccine due to a medical reason, they may be eligible for a preventative medication known as immunoglobulin that can be administered up to six days after exposure, which in this case would be Saturday, Nov. 28. Your primary care physician or the Calhoun County Public Health Department can assist with preventing measles after exposure. People with two, appropriately spaced doses of measles vaccine (MMR) are considered immune. Additionally, adults born before 1957 or those who have evidence of prior measles illness are considered immune. People who are immune who may have been exposed only need to monitor for symptoms after an exposure. The CCPHD recommends that people who are not immune and do not receive MMR or IG to prevent measles after exposure to stay home and avoid all public settings through Dec. 6 for this exposure. This precaution is recommended because people who are not immune could develop measles during the 7-to-21 day incubation period and may be contagious before symptoms appear. The CCPHD is working with Oaklawn Hospital to contact anyone potentially exposed who is not already considered immune. Know the Symptoms: High fever (may spike to over 104 degrees) Cough Runny nose Red, watery eyes (conjunctivitis) Tiny white spots on the inner cheeks, gums and roof of the mouth (Koplik Spots) A rash that starts as flat red spots on the face at the hairline, then spreads to the trunk, arms and legs three to five days after symptoms begin. Small, raised bumps may also appear on top of the flat red spots. More than just a rash Measles complications can include ear infections with hearing loss, pneumonia, brain swelling, premature birth, fetal loss, loss of immunity to other infections, hospitalization and death. The MMR vaccine is considered safe and very effective at protecting against measles, with protection conferred to 93% of persons after one dose and 97% of persons after two doses. There is no county-wide recommendation for vaccinating infants or children earlier than the AAP routine childhood immunization schedule based on local risk assessment at this time. Children should receive their first dose at 12 to 15 months and a second dose at 4 to 6 years. The CCPHD offers MMR vaccinations for children and adults by appointment. Residents who are unsure of their vaccination status can contact their healthcare provider, review their records through Michigan’s immunization registry, or contact CCPHD for assistance. For more information about measles or vaccination appointments, contact the CCHD at 269-969-6363 or visit Michigan.gov/Measles. To learn more about CCPHD services, visit calhouncountymi.gov/publichealth, call 269-969-6370 or find CCPublicHealthDepartment on Instagram or Facebook. Third Wisconsinite dies of measles as cases ramp up - WPR

Skip to content Skip to Audio Player Toggle Site Mobile Navigation Home News Shows Culture Music ABOUT Who We Are Events Careers / Volunteer LISTEN Stations & Schedules Music Playlists Ways to Listen SUPPORT Ways to Give Membership FAQ Funding Information CONTACT Get in Touch Help / FAQ Newsletters POLICIES Ethical Guidelines Policies and Reports Privacy Policy Public Inspection Files Public File Assistance News Shows Culture Music Wisconsin Public Radio Homepage Donate Search wpr.org Health, News Third Wisconsinite dies of measles as cases ramp up Health officials urge people to get vaccinated ahead of holiday gatherings, travel By Hope Kirwan November 24, 2026 Updated November 25, 2026 at 8:15 am Health department staff members enter the Andrews County Health Department measles clinic carrying doses of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine, Tuesday, April 8, 2025, in Andrews, Texas. Earlier this month, health officials reported measles cases in Wisconsin. Annie Rice/AP Photo A third Wisconsinite has died of measles as the highly contagious virus continues to spread unchecked. The state Department of Health Services announced Tuesday an adult in Lafayette County had died. No further details were provided due to privacy concerns. An infant and a child, both from Grant County, died of measles earlier this month, marking the first deaths from the disease since 1990. Understanding Wisconsin, Together. WPR’s “Wisconsin Today” newsletter keeps you connected to the state you love without feeling overwhelmed. No paywall. No agenda. No corporate filter. Instagram This field is for validation purposes and should be left unchanged. Email Measles infections in Wisconsin have spiked in recent weeks. There have now been 918 cases and 19 hospitalizations since August. Nearly all cases have been in people who are unvaccinated. Dr. Ryan Westergaard, chief epidemiologist of communicable diseases at DHS, said unvaccinated people are most at risk as people travel and gather for the Thanksgiving holiday this week. “As we’ve seen with other communicable disease outbreaks, like COVID and flu, social gatherings, particularly indoors, are the way that outbreaks can become accelerated or expanded,” Westergaard said Tuesday on a call with reporters. Two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine in childhood provides lifelong immunity in 97 percent of cases. The two-dose recommendation started in 1989, and some infectious disease experts have questioned whether adults who received one dose in childhood or a less effective form of the shot given during the mid-1960s should get another shot. But Westergaard said Tuesday one dose of the vaccine provides strong protection for about 93 percent of people. “If someone who is in their 30s, 40s or older has gotten a single dose but doesn’t have the records that they received two doses, it is totally safe to get a second dose, and that would increase the probability that they are protected,” he said. “But it is not necessary for most people.” The state has recommended that babies as young as six months who are at risk of exposure receive an early dose of MMR. The shot is generally given between 12 and 15 months to achieve the best immune response. People who are older, have chronic conditions, are pregnant and children under age 5 are most at risk for severe complications from measles. But Westergaard warned that the infection can be severe in anyone and cause pneumonia and swelling of the brain. “It’s not predictable who’s going to get really, very severely ill,” he said. DHS is also tracking instances of measles being passed from a pregnant mother to their baby, what’s called congenital measles. Wisconsin Immunization Program Manager Stephanie Schauer told reporters that 14 people who are or have been pregnant have contracted measles, and there has been one confirmed case of congenital measles. Measles can cause pregnancy loss, premature delivery, and low birth weight in infants, according to the American Academy of Pediatrics. Infants who survive an infection can experience serious neurologic and immune complications for months to years. Schauer said the state has seen an increase in the number of infants receiving an early dose of MMR, which was previously only provided to babies who were traveling out of the country. Wisconsin has reported some of the highest measles activity in the country in recent weeks. Westergaard said Tuesday that the state has requested additional resources from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention through a program called Epi-Aid, which the state previously used in August. “We are going to be receiving additional staff to do case investigation, public communication and management of data,” he said. “We decided to do this now, several months into the outbreak, when it became clear that the outbreak was growing rather than shrinking.” He said the state wanted to ensure counties seeing new cases emerge have the resources they need to respond. Twenty-four of the state’s 72 counties have reported measles cases since August. DHS reported a public exposure to the virus at the Unity Point Health-Meriter emergency department on November 15. It’s the second location in recent months where an unknown number of people have been exposed to the virus. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pennsylvania measles cases reach 1,679 across 46 counties as state adds 41 new infections - Yahoo

News Brief
Pennsylvania measles cases reach 1,679 across 46 counties as state adds 41 new infections The Brief - Pennsylvania health officials reported 41 new measles cases since Nov. 23, pushing the statewide total to 1,679 across 46 counties in 2026. - The outbreak has resulted in 340 hospitalizations and nine confirmed measles-associated deaths so far this year. - Health officials say fewer than 1% of cases occurred in vaccinated residents and continue urging Pennsylvanians to get the two-dose MMR vaccine. PENNSYLVANIA - Pennsylvania health officials announced Wednesday that the state's ongoing measles outbreak has climbed to 1,679 confirmed cases across 46 counties, with 41 new infections reported since Monday. According to the Pennsylvania Department of Health (DOH), the outbreak—which began in late April—has led to 340 hospitalizations and nine confirmed measles-associated deaths so far in 2026. Local perspective State health data shows that nearly all infections have occurred in unvaccinated residents, with only five cases—less than 1% of the total—reported among vaccinated individuals.
Original source text (English)
Pennsylvania measles cases reach 1,679 across 46 counties as state adds 41 new infections The Brief - Pennsylvania health officials reported 41 new measles cases since Nov. 23, pushing the statewide total to 1,679 across 46 counties in 2026. - The outbreak has resulted in 340 hospitalizations and nine confirmed measles-associated deaths so far this year. - Health officials say fewer than 1% of cases occurred in vaccinated residents and continue urging Pennsylvanians to get the two-dose MMR vaccine. PENNSYLVANIA - Pennsylvania health officials announced Wednesday that the state's ongoing measles outbreak has climbed to 1,679 confirmed cases across 46 counties, with 41 new infections reported since Monday. According to the Pennsylvania Department of Health (DOH), the outbreak—which began in late April—has led to 340 hospitalizations and nine confirmed measles-associated deaths so far in 2026. Local perspective State health data shows that nearly all infections have occurred in unvaccinated residents, with only five cases—less than 1% of the total—reported among vaccinated individuals. Since the outbreak started, roughly 300 DOH staff members have worked with local communities and health providers to expand access to the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine: State nurses have administered more than 12,000 MMR doses in 2026, including nearly 7,600 shots across 292 pop-up vaccination clinics. Statewide vaccine demand has surged well above the typical monthly average of 25,000 doses, with providers administering more than 56,000 doses in September, over 62,000 in October and more than 40,000 so far in November. DOH's response team meets every other week with Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) staff to review cases and coordinate containment efforts. Vaccination guidance By the numbers Health officials emphasize that two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% lifetime protection against the virus. Because of ongoing community spread, DOH recommends: - Infants 6 to 11 months old: An early "zero dose" for babies living in or traveling to counties flagged on the state's measles dashboard for sustained transmission. This early shot does not replace the two routine doses. - Children 12 months and older: One dose immediately if unvaccinated, followed by a second dose at least 28 days later. - Adults: Anyone with two documented doses does not need another shot, while adults with only one dose or missing records should consult a health care provider or pharmacist. MMR vaccines are available through primary care providers, county or municipal health departments, DOH State Health Centers, Federally Qualified Health Centers and the Vaccines for Children program. Symptoms and how it spreads What's next Measles is a highly contagious respiratory virus that spreads through breathing, coughing or sneezing, and can linger in the air and on surfaces for up to two hours after an infected person leaves an area. Nearly 20% of Pennsylvanians diagnosed during the outbreak have required hospitalization, and severe complications can include pneumonia and brain swelling. Symptoms typically appear 7 to 21 days after exposure—and people are contagious from four days before until four days after a rash develops. Early signs include: - Fever, cough and runny nose - Red, watery eyes - A rash that starts on the head and spreads downward a few days later Anyone who believes they were exposed and is experiencing symptoms should contact their health care provider or call the state's toll-free hotline at 877-PA-HEALTH (877-724-3258). The Source This article was written using a press release from the Pennsylvania Department of Health
