B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases fall in Almaty region following immunization campaign - Qazinform

News Brief
Health officials attribute the decline to an additional vaccination campaign that immunized 118,000 people during 2024-2025. Patients can transmit the disease for up to four days before the characteristic rash appears. Photo credit: Gov.kz Deputy head of the regional aanitary and epidemiological control department Saulet Kapsalamova said, 5,271 measles cases were recorded in the region in 2023-2024, compared with 727 cases in 2025-2026. Under Kazakhstan’s national immunization schedule, the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine is administered at 12 months and again at six years of age. Health officials are monitoring immunization rates, reviewing the validity of medical exemptions and conducting outreach campaigns among citizens who refuse vaccination.
Original source text (English)
Kazinform Kazakhstan Regions Society Law and Order Culture Sport President Politics President Government Parliament International Relations Economy Analytics World Central Asia Eurasia Middle East America Africa EN KZ QZ РУ EN 中文 ЎЗ ق ز kz qz ру en 中文 ўз ق ز Kazakhstan Regions Society Law and Order Culture Sport President Politics President Government Parliament International Relations Economy Analytics World Central Asia Eurasia Middle East America Africa Measles cases fall in Almaty region following immunization campaign 07:09, 25 November 2026 Measles ncidence in Almaty region decreased by 7.2 times thanks to an additional immunization campaign, although children under the age of five still account for the majority of infections, a Qazinform News Agency correspondent reports. Photo credit: Gov.kz Deputy head of the regional aanitary and epidemiological control department Saulet Kapsalamova said, 5,271 measles cases were recorded in the region in 2023-2024, compared with 727 cases in 2025-2026. Health officials attribute the decline to an additional vaccination campaign that immunized 118,000 people during 2024-2025. Data from sanitary authorities show that 74% of measles patients are children under five years old, while 73% of those infected had not been vaccinated. Among the unvaccinated, 30% were below the eligible vaccination age, 32% had medical exemptions, and 38% were unvaccinated due to parental refusal. Measles is a highly contagious viral infection transmitted through airborne droplets. Among people without immunity, one infected person can spread the virus to up to 18 others. Patients can transmit the disease for up to four days before the characteristic rash appears. Health experts say herd immunity must remain above 95% to prevent outbreaks. “Measles should not be regarded as a mild disease. It can cause serious complications such as pneumonia, ear infections and encephalitis. The infection is particularly dangerous for young children,” Kapsalamova said. She also warned about subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare but severe complication that can develop years after a measles infection and lead to progressive brain damage. Effective treatment for a complete cure is still unavailable. According to specialists, vaccination remains the most effective protection against measles. Under Kazakhstan’s national immunization schedule, the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine is administered at 12 months and again at six years of age. Two doses provide protective immunity in approximately 97-99% of people. Unvaccinated individuals who have been in contact with infected patients are advised to receive emergency vaccination within 72 hours of exposure. This year, 70% of the 16,947 children scheduled for vaccination in Almaty region have received their shots. In addition, 650 healthcare workers were vaccinated, while 3,212 people under 18 received catch-up immunizations. To monitor the epidemiological situation in schools and other organized groups, authorities conducted 101 inspections and imposed 37 administrative measures. The region has also approved a comprehensive 2026-2027 plan aimed at increasing vaccination coverage. Health officials are monitoring immunization rates, reviewing the validity of medical exemptions and conducting outreach campaigns among citizens who refuse vaccination. Earlier, a one-year-old girl died from measles complications in Atyrau region. Health authorities have also reported a sharp rise in measles cases in West Kazakhstan, while the World Health Organization warned this summer about the risk of new outbreaks due to insufficient childhood vaccination coverage. It should be noted, childhood measles vaccination rates have declined globally since the COVID-19 pandemic, contributing to a sharp resurgence of one of the world's most contagious diseases. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Virginia Reported Its First Measles Death in 48 Years. Should You Be Concerned? - Northern Virginia Magazine

News Brief
Virginia reported its first measles death in 48 years, with 220 cases this year. The majority of new cases are homegrown and not related to international travel. Measles is highly contagious, with 90% of people exposed in a room with one infected person. Symptoms include a rash, fever, and cough. Health officials advise getting a titer test to check immunity and getting a booster if needed.
Original source text (English)
The Virginia Department of Health last week announced the state’s first measles death in 48 years — a child under the age of 4. And the nation has lost the measles-elimination status that it gained in 2000, as cases continue to grow nationwide. Virginia had four measles cases in 2025 , according to the Center for Infectious Disease Research and Policy at the University of Minnesota. So far this year, the number is 220. VDH says the vast majority of new cases are homegrown and not related to international travel. And that all new cases since October 17 of this year have been in people who were not vaccinated or whose vaccination status was unknown. Nicole Dickinson RN, director of the emergency department at Sentara Northern Virginia, says they haven’t seen a significant uptick in measles cases. And that people should hold tight to the preventative procedures they’ve already taken. If you have any symptoms of the measles, she says, follow up with your primary care professional. And she repeated the most important advice for dealing with almost any communicable disease: “If you’re sick, stay home.” Here’s what Dr. Sujata Ambardar , an infectious disease physician at Inova Fairfax Hospital, wants people in Northern Virginia to know: What Causes Measles Outbreaks? The most obvious explanation for the outbreaks across the country is growing resistance to vaccines. Naturally, a vaccination is Ambardar’s first suggestion for how people should protect themselves. She adds that measles is more contagious than most other airborne infections. “If you have one person with measles in a room, 90 percent of people will get exposed to measles,” she says. That’s much higher than, for example, tuberculosis. That means a mask isn’t going to help much against the measles, she adds. A rash, fever, and/or a cough can be the early symptoms of measles . Rhinitis (nasal congestion and itching) and conjunctivitis can also be signs of the disease. “If there is anybody that has symptoms that you see, then you need to be extremely cautious and would try to obviously not be near them,” Ambardar says. Fortunately, in this area most jurisdictions have “historically been very good about mandating vaccines,” Ambardar says. “And in fact, most colleges won’t let you attend unless you’ve been vaccinated for certain things like measles, mumps, rubella, tetanus, Tdap, and even some of the meningococcal vaccines.” Dickinson advises consulting with your doctor. “Comorbidities do play a role, and people should follow up with practitioners who know them,” she says Do I Need a Booster? Probably not, Ambardar says. She has heard the reports that some people who got vaccinated between 1957 and 1968 “may have gotten vaccines that were inactivated — not a live vaccine — so those people may not have as much of a robust response.” That doesn’t mean you should go running out for a booster, she says. If you’re truly concerned about your protection against measles infection, she recommends getting a titer test. A titer is a blood test that will show whether you have immunity, either through vaccination or by previously having had the disease. “I actually keep [mine] in my desk,” Ambardar says, “because it’s positive, so I never have to get tested again. I would never need a booster.” The bar may be higher for health care workers, she says. They should get their titers checked to be sure. But for the general population, if you’ve been safe, you still should be. “Only if the blood test is negative would I get a booster,” Ambardar says. “Not because it’s an unsafe vaccine, but why get a vaccine if you don’t need it?” “The only time they recommend a booster, they’re saying, is if there’s an outbreak ongoing,” Ambaradar says. If you have incomplete protection and an outbreak is raging, they won’t want to wait until everyone’s titers come back from the lab. “But we’re not in that situation here in the DMV, so I think it’s better just to get a titer if there’s any concern.” Get can’t-miss updates delivered directly to your inbox with our NoVA Daily newsletter

Virginia, United States.
Event
Virginia reported its first measles death in 48 years, and the number of measles cases has increased to 220 this year. The Virginia Department of Health (VDH) states that the majority of new cases are homegrown and not related to international travel.
Scale, impact and risk
There have been four measles cases in Virginia in 2025, and 220 cases so far this year. The disease is highly contagious, with 90% of people exposed in a room with one infected person. Symptoms include a rash, fever, and cough. The risk is higher for unvaccinated individuals.
Response, status and uncertainty
VDH is monitoring the situation and advises people to follow preventative procedures. They recommend getting a titer test to check immunity and getting a booster if the test is negative. Health care workers should get their titers checked to ensure they are protected.

世卫组织警告称,疫苗 缺口助长全球 麻疹 死灰复燃

Vaccine gaps fuelling global measles resurgence, WHO warns
新闻简报Poor vaccination coverage is fuelling a resurgence in measles outbreaks globally, the World Health Organization (WHO) has warned. Although measles deaths are at a record low, 29 million children were left unvaccinated or under-vaccinated in 2025, according to the UN health body's annual assessment of global progress towards eliminating measles, released on Friday. Earlier this month, the United States, Mexico and Bolivia lost their measles elimination status , after the Pan American Health Organization, the WHO's regional agency for the Americas, found the virus had been circulating continuously in each country for at least 12 months. The UK lost its status in January . "Elimination is not the finish line," Dr Kate O'Brien, the WHO's director of immunisation, told reporters at a press briefing to mark the launch of the report on Wednesday. "The recent setbacks we've seen are not a failure of the measles vaccine; they're a failure to reach every child with the vaccine". Measles is one of the most contagious viruses known to humans and requires 95 per cent of the community to be vaccinated to prevent outbreaks.
查看新闻五要素
时间
世界卫生组织免疫主管凯特·奥布莱恩博士在周三发布该报告的新闻发布会上对记者说:“消灭并不是终点线。”
地点与对象
2025 年,59 个国家出现“大规模或破坏性 暴发”,定义为 12 个月内每百万人病例超过 20 例。
事件
There were nearly 10 million cases in 2025, down 74 per cent from about 38 million in 2000, one of the lowest levels ever recorded.
规模、影响与风险
疫苗 联盟首席 疫苗 项目和市场官 An Vermeersch 表示:“2025 年 麻疹 死亡人数创历史新低,有力地提醒人们,持续投资免疫接种可以取得什么成果。”
应对、状态与不确定性
约翰内斯堡(美联社)——在南非一个温暖的春天的早晨,就在结账前,一只孤独的蜜蜂在世界上最小的酒店之一外面偷看,然后又退回到自己的房间:在未经处理的松树上钻出的一个小洞。监控这只黑色小客人的是约翰内斯堡的西莉亚·豪普特(Celia Haupt),她是一名银行分析师兼蜜蜂酒店经营者,她拥有分布在她所在城市和开普敦各地志愿者的 510 家蜜蜂酒店之一。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Vaccine gaps fuelling global measles resurgence, WHO warns

News Brief
Poor vaccination coverage is fuelling a resurgence in measles outbreaks globally, the World Health Organization (WHO) has warned. Although measles deaths are at a record low, 29 million children were left unvaccinated or under-vaccinated in 2025, according to the UN health body's annual assessment of global progress towards eliminating measles, released on Friday. Earlier this month, the United States, Mexico and Bolivia lost their measles elimination status , after the Pan American Health Organization, the WHO's regional agency for the Americas, found the virus had been circulating continuously in each country for at least 12 months. The UK lost its status in January . "Elimination is not the finish line," Dr Kate O'Brien, the WHO's director of immunisation, told reporters at a press briefing to mark the launch of the report on Wednesday. "The recent setbacks we've seen are not a failure of the measles vaccine; they're a failure to reach every child with the vaccine". Measles is one of the most contagious viruses known to humans and requires 95 per cent of the community to be vaccinated to prevent outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
Poor vaccination coverage is fuelling a resurgence in measles outbreaks globally, the World Health Organization (WHO) has warned. Although measles deaths are at a record low, 29 million children were left unvaccinated or under-vaccinated in 2025, according to the UN health body's annual assessment of global progress towards eliminating measles, released on Friday. Earlier this month, the United States, Mexico and Bolivia lost their measles elimination status , after the Pan American Health Organization, the WHO's regional agency for the Americas, found the virus had been circulating continuously in each country for at least 12 months. The UK lost its status in January . "Elimination is not the finish line," Dr Kate O'Brien, the WHO's director of immunisation, told reporters at a press briefing to mark the launch of the report on Wednesday. "The recent setbacks we've seen are not a failure of the measles vaccine; they're a failure to reach every child with the vaccine". Measles is one of the most contagious viruses known to humans and requires 95 per cent of the community to be vaccinated to prevent outbreaks. Children under-five, pregnant women and people with weakened immune systems are at greatest risk. According to the WHO, it can cause pneumonia, brain infection, deafness and blindness, death and "immune amnesia", where the virus can weaken the immune system for months or even years. The measles vaccine, which used a weakened (live attenuated) virus in two doses, is safe and highly effective. Dr O'Brien said: "Some of the more recent graduates have never seen a case of measles, but it is back". Diana Chang Blanc, unit head of the essential programme on immunisation at the WHO, said the spread of cases across income levels showed that even in places with strong health systems, small drops in coverage can leave pockets of unvaccinated people that can spread risks far beyond those areas. In 2025, 59 countries had "large or disruptive outbreaks", defined as more than 20 cases per million people over 12 months. Of them, 11 were previously declared measles-free. The US was not among them, as its outbreaks didn't reach the threshold. "It can take years to regain elimination status, whereas it takes only a 12-month period to lose elimination status," said Dr O'Brien. "These are hard-fought gains that countries have made, and protecting them now is more important than ever". Outbreaks were also not confined to poorer nations: 18 were in low-income countries, 34 in middle-income countries and seven in high-income countries. Dr O'Brien, when asked about messaging from the US government, did not name any country but said vaccines had become "tangled up" with political and ideological polarisation in many places. In the US, the loss of measles-free status follows nearly two years of Donald Trump and Robert F Kennedy Jr, his health secretary, casting doubt on vaccine safety . In August, Mr Trump called for the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) combination vaccine to be split into separate shots, arguing without evidence that "together, there could be a possibility they're quite lethal". Ms Chang Blanc said barriers to access are also a major issue, including distance to clinics, transport costs, time off work, vaccine shortages and families being unaware about the second dose. "Measles circulation anywhere threatens gains achieved elsewhere. So, achieving a measles-free world is only possible if every child, everywhere, has access to and is fully protected with the benefit of immunisation," she added. There have been some major gains in tackling the infection this century, the report says. Measles deaths are down 91 per cent, from about 770,000 in 2000 to roughly 72,000 in 2025. There were nearly 10 million cases in 2025, down 74 per cent from about 38 million in 2000, one of the lowest levels ever recorded. Vaccination has been key to this: the measles jab has saved more than 60 million lives since 2000. The "Big Catch Up", launched in April 2023 by the WHO, Unicef and Gavi to help children get routine vaccination they missed during the Covid-19 pandemic, also gave more than 17 million children their first dose. But coverage still hasn't fully recovered. In 2025, 84 per cent of children got a first dose and 77 per cent got a second, very short of the 95 per cent needed to stop the virus spreading. Before the pandemic, first-dose coverage was 86 per cent. "These immunity gaps don't disappear on their own; they grow cumulatively larger every single year, and the outbreaks that we're seeing today are the predictable consequence of these gaps," said Dr O'Brien. "Are we ringing the alarm bell about the number of children who are unvaccinated or under vaccinated? Absolutely". Eight in 10 unimmunised children live in the Africa or Eastern Mediterranean regions. The African region, home to 16 per cent of the world's population, accounts for almost 40 per cent of measles deaths. The South-East Asia region has about 23 per cent of the world's population, but accounts for nearly half of global cases. It has the highest coverage of any region (96 per cent for the first dose and 93 per cent for the second), but its population is so large that even small dips in coverage leave millions of children unprotected. The Americas is the only region where cases have risen since 2000, reaching about 73,000 in 2025, about eight times the figure at the turn of the century. "The record-low number of measles deaths in 2025 is a powerful reminder of what sustained investment in immunisation can achieve," said An Vermeersch, Chief Vaccine Programmes & Markets Officer at Gavi, the Vaccine Alliance. "This progress is also a reminder of the work ahead. Millions of children remain unvaccinated, leaving them vulnerable to measles outbreaks and preventable illness and death," she said. New Ohio measles cases this week nearly match total from 2025

News Brief
Find us on Google 📌 America's birthday 🎂 Start the day smarter ☀️ Get the USA TODAY app U.S. Politics Sports Entertainment Life Money Travel Opinion Crossword ONLY AT USA TODAY: Newsletters For Subscribers Real Estate From the Archives USA TODAY PLAY Crossword Marvel Comics eNewspaper Magazines Investigations Video Podcasts Humankind Advertise Your Business WITNESS (True Crime) Just Curious Best-selling Booklist Top Workplaces Nomination Legals Press Releases Cars Boats Grocery Shopping Holidays OUR PORTFOLIO: Real Estate 10BEST USA TODAY Store Shopping Money Essentials WITNESS (True Crime) Southern Kitchen Home Internet Services HEALTH World Health Organization Add Topic New Ohio measles cases this week nearly match total from 2025 Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Nov. 27, 2026, 10:47 a.m. ET Hear this story Ohio is experiencing its third measles outbreak of 2026, with 400 cases reported in 30 counties so far this year. Original source text (English)
Find us on Google 📌 America's birthday 🎂 Start the day smarter ☀️ Get the USA TODAY app U.S. Politics Sports Entertainment Life Money Travel Opinion Crossword ONLY AT USA TODAY: Newsletters For Subscribers Real Estate From the Archives USA TODAY PLAY Crossword Marvel Comics eNewspaper Magazines Investigations Video Podcasts Humankind Advertise Your Business WITNESS (True Crime) Just Curious Best-selling Booklist Top Workplaces Nomination Legals Press Releases Cars Boats Grocery Shopping Holidays OUR PORTFOLIO: Real Estate 10BEST USA TODAY Store Shopping Money Essentials WITNESS (True Crime) Southern Kitchen Home Internet Services HEALTH World Health Organization Add Topic New Ohio measles cases this week nearly match total from 2025 Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Nov. 27, 2026, 10:47 a.m. ET Hear this story Ohio is experiencing its third measles outbreak of 2026, with 400 cases reported in 30 counties so far this year. The majority of measles cases in Ohio are among unvaccinated individuals, as vaccination rates have fallen below the herd immunity threshold. Nationally, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, causing the U.S. to lose the measles elimination status it had held since 2000. Ohio's measles case count so far in 2026 is nearly three times greater than the numbers from the previous four years combined. On the heels of one person potentially spreading measles across five locations in Western Ohio, which happened roughly a week after a similar situation in Geauga and Lake counties, two more counties joined the list of confirmed cases as the state's third measles outbreak of 2026 continues. ODH is reporting 400 cases in 30 of 88 Ohio counties so far this year, according to its summary of infectious diseases dashboard, updated Nov. 26. That number is nearly eight times (788%) more than the 45 cases reported in all of 2025. Ohio added 42 new cases in the past week, a 35% increase from the 31 new cases reported the week before, the Ohio Department of Health reports. Coshocton and Monroe counties joined the list this week with three cases total. Ashland, Medina and Wayne counties had the most new cases. Nationally, the United States has officially lost its measles-free status. The disease had been declared eradicated from the US in 2000 in what the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention called a "historic" accomplishment. Measles cases nationwide have hit a 35-year high. As of Nov. 24, Johns Hopkins University counted 6,369 measles cases in the U.S. in 2026, and as of Nov. 25, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported 6,038 confirmed measles cases in 47 states plus Washington, D.C. – more than double last year's total of 2,289, USA TODAY reports. That's the highest annual total since 1991. The vast majority of cases in Ohio are among unvaccinated individuals, per ODH. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Measles growing in Ohio. These counties report cases in 2026 Ohio counties reporting measles cases since Jan. 1, 2026, include: Ashland: 13 (five new cases) Ashtabula: 58 Coshocton: 2 (first time on list) Cuyahoga: 3 Franklin: 8 Gallia: 7 (four new cases) Geauga: 69 (four new cases) Hancock: 1 Harrison: 18 Holmes: 15 (one new case) Jackson: 1 Knox: 5 Mahoning: 2 Medina: 40 (12 new cases) Meigs: 4 Mercer: 8 (four new cases) Monroe: 1 (first time on list) Morgan: 3 Morrow: 13 Ottawa: 1 Paulding: 1 Pike: 27 (one new case) Portage: 1 Stark: 1 Trumbull: 19 (three new cases) Tuscarawas: 15 Van Wert: 3 Vinton: 1 Wayne: 59 (five new cases) Wood: 1 The case count for the year reflects previous outbreaks in Cuyahoga County in January and Franklin County in February. The ages of those with confirmed cases range from 15 months to 20 years old, according to various health departments. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with one case in 2023, seven in 2024 and 45 cases in 2025. When including the 90 measles cases in 2022, Ohio's total so far in 2026 is nearly three times greater than the previous four years combined. Weekly Ohio measles cases by the numbers Total cases in Ohio this year have continued climbing, as reported by ODH: 358 cases in 28 counties on Nov. 19 327 cases in 26 counties on Nov. 12 310 cases in 25 counties on Nov. 5 296 cases in 24 counties on Oct. 29 260 cases in 24 counties on Oct. 22 252 cases in 23 counties on Oct. 15 228 cases in 21 counties on Oct. 8 206 cases in 20 counties on Oct. 1 196 cases in 16 counties on Sept. 24 180 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 17 133 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 10 122 cases in 14 counties on Sept. 3 107 cases in 12 counties on Aug. 27 99 cases in 12 counties Aug. 20 69 cases in 11 counties on Aug. 13 45 cases in 10 counties on Aug. 6 Measles kills at least nine in Pennsylvania; infant dies of measles in Wisconsin Pennsylvania confirmed its seventh measles-related death of the year on Nov. 16, while New York announced its first measles death in 30 years on Nov. 2, according to USA TODAY. As of Nov. 25, the CDC reports nine measles deaths in the U.S. in 2026. Wisconsin also reported a grim milestone on Nov. 5, with health officials saying that its first measles death since 1990 was that of an infant. Reuters on Nov. 25 reports 14 measles deaths in the U.S. this year: Nine deaths in Pennsylvania, three in Wisconsin, and one each in New York and Virginia. Measles cases on the rise as Ohio vaccination rates fluctuate in state, U.S. Vaccination rates for measles have declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Ohio's vaccination rate for the 2025-26 school year slipped well below the herd immunity threshold, making it susceptible to outbreaks. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventive measures, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of a 2025 Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine. Trump signs executive order to separate MMR vaccine President Donald Trump in August 2026 signed an executive order to split the childhood measles, mumps and rubella vaccine into three shots and delivering them at separate medical visits as well as "calling for more vaccine flexibility and research" and fewer childhood vaccinations, according to USA TODAY. "You have the MMR, we want it in three separate vaccinations, given at separate times. Together there could be a possibility they're quite lethal and separately, it looks like they are not at all lethal, but just very effective," said Trump from the Oval Office as he signed the order. But the MMR vaccine is not lethal, and medical research shows no health benefit or reduced risk from splitting it. In contrast, separating the shots increases injection pain, leaves children unprotected longer, and lowers overall vaccination rates, according to experts. The MMR vaccine has been in existence since 1971, according to the World Health Organization. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States, country loses 'eliminated' status The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. The United States has officially lost its measles-free status three decades after the "historic public health achievement" that eliminated the highly infectious disease from the country, the Pan American Health Organization, the part of the World Health Organization that tracks infectious disease in the Americas, announced Nov. 17. Defined by the WHO as freedom from the continuous spread of a disease for at least 12 months, the United States achieved measles elimination status in 2000 in what the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention called a "historic" accomplishment. Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, director of PAHO, pointed to gaps in vaccination coverage as a significant culprit. Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but they can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before through four days after a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. Featured Weekly Ad

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles deaths hit historic low, but US, Mexico and Bolivia lose elimination status - UN News

News Brief
The World Health Organization (WHO) reported that measles deaths have reached a historic low, but the virus is regaining a foothold in the United States, Mexico, and Bolivia, leading to the loss of their measles elimination status. This development highlights a concerning trend in these countries, where measles is re-emerging despite global efforts to reduce its impact. The WHO's statement confirms the resurgence, but it does not provide specific case or death counts. The situation is under investigation, and the implications suggest a need for renewed public health measures to prevent further spread. The report was published on 2026-11-27, indicating that the findings are current and based on the latest available data.

The article from ABC News dated 2026-11-25 reports that the United States has lost its measles elimination status, raising concerns about the potential resurgence of the disease. This development highlights the importance of maintaining high vaccination rates to prevent outbreaks. The piece emphasizes the need for vaccine boosters, particularly for parents, as the country faces a risk of increased measles cases. However, the article does not provide specific numbers on cases, deaths, or exposures, nor does it detail current public health responses or investigations. The loss of elimination status is a significant public health concern, as it indicates that measles is no longer effectively controlled within the country, potentially leading to new outbreaks and challenges in disease management.

宾夕法尼亚州记录了第 9 例 麻疹 死亡病例,该州 麻疹 病例超过 1,600 例 - 哥伦比亚广播公司新闻

9th measles death recorded in Pennsylvania as outbreak surpasses 1,600 cases in the state - CBS News
新闻简报宾夕法尼亚州记录了今年第九起与 麻疹 相关的死亡,该州的 麻疹 病例超过 1,600 例,创下数十年来的最高数字。中心县报告了这起死亡事件,该州卫生部指出,这是宾夕法尼亚州 35 年来首例与 麻疹 相关的死亡事件。据CDC称,截至上周,美国麻疹确诊病例已激增至5,838例。暴发 归因于自 2025 年以来病例数创 30 年来新高,专家们强调该病毒的传播以及对弱势群体的风险,包括免疫功能低下的个体和太小而无法接种疫苗的婴儿。传染病专家埃文·希雷 (Evan Shirey) 对 疫苗 的犹豫不决表示担忧,并指出错误信息是一个促成因素。该州卫生官员尚未提供有关已故患者的更多详细信息,但他们强调了 疫苗接种 对预防这种疾病的重要性。麻疹 强调了维持 麻疹 淘汰地位的挑战,美国上周失去了这一地位。

时间
2026 年 11 月 23 日,宾夕法尼亚州中心县报告了第九起与 麻疹 相关的死亡事件,当时 麻疹 达到了几十年来的最高水平。据 CDC 称,截至上周,麻疹 确诊病例激增至 5,838 例。

该事件发生在宾夕法尼亚州中心县,暴发 影响了整个州。暴发 是该国最大的一次,影响了很大一部分人口,包括儿童和免疫功能低下的个人。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

9th measles death recorded in Pennsylvania as outbreak surpasses 1,600 cases in the state - CBS News

News Brief
Pennsylvania has recorded its ninth measles-related death of the year, as the state's outbreak surpasses 1,600 cases, marking the highest number in decades. The death was reported in Centre County, and the state's Department of Health noted that this is the first measles-related death in Pennsylvania in 35 years. The U.S. measles cases have surged to 5,838 confirmed cases as of last week, according to the CDC. The outbreak is attributed to a 30-year high in cases from 2025, and experts are highlighting the spread of the virus and the risk to vulnerable populations, including immunocompromised individuals and infants too young to be vaccinated. Evan Shirey, an infectious disease specialist, expressed concern over vaccine hesitancy, citing misinformation as a contributing factor. The state's health officials have not provided further details about the deceased patient, but they emphasize the importance of vaccination to prevent the disease. The outbreak underscores the challenges in maintaining measles elimination status, which the U.S. lost last week.
Original source text (English)

Luciano is the recipient of multiple journalism awards, including a Walter Cronkite Award, a regional Edward R. Murrow Award and five regional Emmys. Read Full Bio Lilia Luciano November 23, 2026 / 8:26 PM EST / CBS News Add CBS News on Google Pennsylvania on Monday announced its ninth measles-related death this year as cases continue to surge across the U.S., reaching their highest mark in decades. The death was reported in Centre County, in the middle of the state. The Pennsylvania Department of Health did not provide any further information about the patient. The announcement came just three days after Virginia reported that a child under the age of 5 had died after testing positive for measles. It was Virginia's first measles-related death this year, the state's Health Department said. Pennsylvania's outbreak is the largest in the country, with over 1,600 cases reported, according to the state Department of Health, and the deaths this year are the Keystone State's first from measles in 35 years. In Lancaster County, one woman told CBS News her three children all have measles. The U.S. had eliminated measles by the year 2000, but last week the country lost that status. A country is considered to have eliminated measles if there are "no locally transmitted measles infections or outbreaks lasting 12 months or longer," according to the World Health Organization. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention data show that about a quarter of this year's measles cases are linked to outbreaks that began in 2025, when the U.S. saw a 30-year high of 2,289 cases, and that the virus continued to spread. As of last week, there have been 5,838 confirmed measles cases in the U.S. this year, according to the CDC. Dr. Evan Shirey, an infectious disease specialist, said he went from seeing no measles cases to dealing with them "almost every day." 1/1 Skip Ad Continue watchingafter the adVisit Advertiser websiteGO TO PAGE He blames misinformation for vaccine hesitancy. "The manifestations of that can just be horrible to see," he said. "Children gasping for air. Seeing a child who is altered, confused, unable to recognize their own mom." Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has long been criticized for casting doubt on vaccine safety, said in the wake of the U.S. losing its measles elimination status, "I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles." Kindergarten vaccination rates have fallen below the 95% needed to protect the immunocompromised and infants too young to be vaccinated. Among the latter group: Shirey's 3-month-old son. "I'm absolutely counting down the days," he told CBS News. "I'll have him in there on the date to make sure that I'm doing what I can for him to protect him." The first dose of the MMR vaccine is usually given at 12 to 15 months of age, but can be given as early as 6 months in high-risk cases, such as during an ongoing outbreak. Kerry Breen and Grace Manthey contributed to this report. 感恩节旅行引发对 麻疹 传播的担忧 - 新闻周刊

Thanksgiving Travel Raises Concern Over Measles Spread - Newsweek
新闻简报随着数百万美国人感恩节旅行,健康专家对美国可能出现的 麻疹 暴发 表示担忧。美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 报告,截至 2026 年 11 月 19 日,已有 5,838 例病例,这一数字已是 2025 年的两倍。此后已有 10 名患者死亡,其中至少 5 名是儿童,这是自 1991 年以来 麻疹 死亡人数最多的一年。该病毒通过空气传播,具有高度传染性,尤其是在未接种疫苗的个体中。症状通常在接触后两周出现,首先是高烧和皮疹,并可能导致失明和脑肿胀等严重并发症。Alexis Jesup 博士和 Kanai Kim 博士建议在拥挤的地方戴口罩,如果身体不适,请留在家里,并为前往 暴发 地区的儿童咨询儿科医生。暴发 正在进行中,CDC 正在监测情况。美洲自 2016 年以来一直没有 麻疹,但最近的案例表明进展出现了逆转。由于病毒能够在未接种疫苗的社区中迅速传播,尤其是在假日旅行和大型聚会期间,风险仍然很高。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至2026年11月19日,活动日期为《暴发》发布时间,发布日期为2026年11月24日。
地点与对象
美国,重点关注 麻疹 麻疹 发生的地区以及未接种疫苗的人群。
事件
美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 报告称,截至 2026 年 11 月 19 日,已有 5,838 例病例,健康专家警告称,随着数百万美国人回家过感恩节,可能会出现 麻疹 麻疹 暴发。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已导致 10 人死亡,其中至少有 5 人是儿童,这是自 1991 年以来死亡人数最多的一年。该病毒主要通过未接种疫苗的人传播,会造成失明和脑肿胀等严重并发症。
应对、状态与不确定性
卫生官员建议未接种疫苗的人如果生病了待在家里,在拥挤的地方戴口罩,并为前往 暴发 地区的儿童咨询儿科医生。CDC 正在监测情况,暴发 仍在进行中,尚未确定解决方案。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Thanksgiving Travel Raises Concern Over Measles Spread - Newsweek

News Brief
Health experts are raising concerns about a potential measles outbreak in the United States as millions of Americans travel for Thanksgiving. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported 5,838 cases as of November 19, 2026, already double the number from 2025. Ten patients have since died, with at least five being children, marking the deadliest year for measles since 1991. The virus spreads through the air and is highly contagious, particularly among unvaccinated individuals. Symptoms typically develop two weeks after exposure, starting with a high fever and rash, and can lead to serious complications such as blindness and brain swelling. Dr. Alexis Jesup and Dr. Kanai Kim recommend wearing masks in crowded spaces, staying home if unwell, and consulting pediatricians for children traveling to outbreak areas. The outbreak is ongoing, and the CDC is monitoring the situation. The Americas had been measles-free since 2016, but recent cases suggest a reversal of progress. The risk remains high due to the virus's ability to spread rapidly in unvaccinated communities, especially during holiday travel and large gatherings.
Original source text (English)
U.S. News Politics AI Politics Tech Fact Check Sports Global Iran War Russia-Ukraine Middle East China And Asia Live Blog Better Planet All World News Lifestyle Family & Parenting Entertainment Travel Pets Automotive Relationships Science Health Personal Finance Better Workplaces Readers Choice Opinion My Turn All Opinion Rankings Health Finance Products Education Workplaces Corporate Responsibility All Rankings Watch The 1600 The Royal Report Unconventional From The Paddock Newsmakers Extra Newsletters Dare to Dream Events Essays Podcasts Vantage Insights Magazine CEO Circle Announcements Leadership Dev Pragad Subscribe Sign In U.S. NewsPoliticsAI PoliticsTechFact CheckSports Global Iran WarRussia-UkraineMiddle EastChina And AsiaLive BlogBetter PlanetAll World News Lifestyle Family & ParentingEntertainmentTravelPetsAutomotiveRelationshipsScienceHealthPersonal FinanceBetter WorkplacesReaders Choice Opinion My TurnAll Opinion Rankings HealthCorporate ResponsibilityFinanceProductsEducationWorkAll Rankings Watch The 1600The Royal ReportUnconventionalFrom The PaddockNewsmakers Social FacebookInstagramYouTubeRedditTikTokTwitter/X Extra NewslettersDare to DreamEventsEssaysPodcastsVantageInsightsMagazineCEO CircleAnnouncementsLeadershipDev Pragad Health experts are warning of a potential measles outbreak as millions of Americans travel home for Thanksgiving. The U.S. lost its measles-free status last week after 26 years, with 5,838 cases reported by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) as of November 19 - already double the number in 2025. Ten patients subsequently died, at least five of whom were children, marking the deadliest year since 1991 as the highly contagious virus spreads mainly through the unvaccinated. Measles spreads through the air, and a single infected person can spread the virus to as many as 18 people, making crowded airports and holiday celebrations high-risk areas of exposure. Symptoms typically develop about two weeks after exposure and begin with a high fever followed by a rash that starts on the face and neck. Other signs include fatigue, nasal congestion, and pink eye, and serious complications could lead to blindness and swelling of the brain. The virus currently exists in pockets of the country, but Thanksgiving travel and large family gatherings present a chance for it to spread. Thanksgiving Travel Advice Dr. Alexis Jesup, a family physician with Roper St. Francis Healthcare, recommended wearing a mask while traveling in crowded spaces or around people who are sick, and to stay home if you feel ill. She said: "If you’re not feeling good, stay home. Do a FaceTime, do a video chat with your family to celebrate, and see your doctor if you’re not feeling well." Dr. Kanai Kim, an infectious disease specialist with USF and Tampa General Hospital, added that families should be mindful of where they are traveling, whether it's experiencing an outbreak, and who they are gathering with. She told Fox 13: "Most people haven't seen a case of measles. So they don't understand how bad measles can be... If you've got a vulnerable person like your baby, it's so hard to protect them. It just sweeps through the community." Dr. Kim said parents traveling with their children to areas experiencing measles outbreaks should consult their pediatricians, as an early dose of the vaccine may be appropriate for babies between six and 11 months. Importance of Vaccines In the last two years, over 7,000 measles cases and 13 deaths have been reported - nearly all of whom were either unvaccinated or didn't report whether they were vaccinated. More than 10 percent of cases resulted in hospitalization. Two doses of the 97 percent effective measles mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine usually protect people for life, as does having previously had the disease. Unvaccinated children also risk contracting subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a condition caused by the measles virus destroying brain cells that can start years after an infection but cannot be triggered by the vaccine. It can change a child's personality and cause seizures of involuntary movements, which lead to dementia and problems eating and breathing. It is almost always fatal, but the best way to prevent it is with the measles vaccine. Dr. Jesup said: "Vaccines are available to adults, adolescents, all people at any point in time... If you’re not vaccinated and you have the opportunity, get it." The Americas became the first region in the world to be free of measles in 2016 following a hugely successful vaccination campaign that health officials estimate prevented 6.2 million deaths between 2000 and 2023. B routine priorityPublic-health news

The US has lost its measles elimination status - Harvard Health

News Brief
The article from Harvard Health, published on November 23, 2026, reports that the United States has lost its measles elimination status. This means that measles is no longer absent in the country, indicating a resurgence of the disease. The exact number of cases, deaths, or exposures is not specified in the evidence, but the loss of elimination status suggests a potential public health risk. The report does not provide details about the investigation, testing, or response measures taken, which are necessary for a comprehensive understanding of the situation. The source is considered reputable, and the information is confirmed based on the report's content. The implications of this status change highlight the need for increased vigilance and public health measures to control the spread of measles.

How to Protect Your Baby During a Measles Outbreak: FAQs - HealthyChildren.org

News Brief
This article from HealthyChildren.org, published on November 24, 2026, offers general guidance on how to protect babies during a measles outbreak. It emphasizes the importance of vaccination, maintaining good hygiene, and avoiding exposure to infected individuals. The article does not provide specific data on cases, deaths, or exposures, nor does it detail any investigation or response activities. Instead, it serves as a preventive resource for parents and caregivers, highlighting measures to safeguard infants in the context of a measles outbreak. The content is intended to inform and educate rather than report on an active outbreak or its impact. The article does not mention any specific location or population affected by the outbreak, focusing instead on broad recommendations for public health preparedness.

随着 麻疹 的回归,实验室需要可靠的诊断参考 - 科学家

As Measles Returns, Labs Need Reliable Diagnostic References - The Scientist
新闻简报文章讨论了 麻疹 在美国的死灰复燃,该国由于免疫率下降而失去了 麻疹 消除状态。这导致该病毒成为地方性流行病,在人群中传播。这篇文章强调了实验室拥有可靠的诊断参考以确保准确和一致的测试结果的重要性。然而,它没有提供具体的病例数、死亡人数或公共卫生当局为解决 暴发 问题而采取的措施的详细信息。重点是在 麻疹 病例数量不断增加的情况下,诊断实验室在保持测试准确性方面面临的挑战。

时间
2026 年 11 月 24 日 | 3 分钟阅读 滴鼻剂为大脑提供 mRNA 疗法 前 NIH 神经科学家被撤回 32 项美国移民官员拘留了一名研究生 2026 年 11 月 27 日 | 4 分钟阅读 当人工智能生物技术公司失败时,药物专利会发生什么?
地点与对象
随着 麻疹 的回归,实验室需要可靠的诊断参考作者:Leka Papazisi,DVM,博士 | 2026 年 11 月 24 日 |阅读 5 分钟随着 麻疹 病例的增加以及越来越多的实验室建立自己的诊断测试,充分表征的参考材料可以使结果准确可靠。
事件
由于免疫率下降导致病例增加,导致 麻疹病毒 流行,美国已失去 麻疹 消除状态。
规模、影响与风险
麻疹 病例正在攀升,但所提供的证据中没有报告具体的病例数、死亡人数或暴露人数。该风险被明确表述为在该国境内流行。
应对、状态与不确定性
文章强调,随着 麻疹 病例的增加,需要可靠的诊断参考,但它没有报告当局采取的具体调查、测试或遏制措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

As Measles Returns, Labs Need Reliable Diagnostic References - The Scientist

News Brief
The article discusses the resurgence of measles in the United States, where the country has lost its measles elimination status due to a decline in immunization rates. This has led to the virus becoming endemic, spreading within the population. The piece emphasizes the importance of having reliable diagnostic references for labs to ensure accurate and consistent testing results. However, it does not provide specific case counts, death tolls, or details on the measures taken by public health authorities to address the outbreak. The focus is on the challenges faced by diagnostic laboratories in maintaining test accuracy amid the rising number of measles cases.
Original source text (English)

Skip to main content Download the AppGet your news faster with our mobile experience Install News Weather Features Eyewitness Mornings Chime In Watch Live Now 58° Sat 67° Sun 70° Sign In or Create Account Watch Kern County doctors urge vaccine checks ahead of Thanksgiving travel amid measles risk by Caroline Rowland Mon, November 23, 2026 at 11:45 AM Play/Pause Video Quality Speed0.25x0.5xnormal1.5x2x Audio Track Audio Quality Subtitlesopen Font sizedefault50%75%100%150%200%300%400% Font styledefaultitalicbold Font familydefaultmonospaced serifproportional serifmonospaced sans serifproportional sans serifcasualcursivesmall capital Font colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Font opacitydefault100%75%50%25% Character edgedefaultraiseddepresseduniformdrop shadowed Character edge colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Background colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Background opacitydefault100%75%50%25%0% Window colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Window opacitydefault100%75%50%25%0% BackReset 00:01 00:00 PlayMute Picture-in-PictureApple AirPlayGoogle CastVRSettingsFullscreen Replay logo 3VIEW ALL PHOTOS FILE - A nurse, prepares a combination measles, mumps and rubella vaccine for a patient at a pediatrics clinic in Easley, S.C., March 17, 2026. (AP Photo/Mary Conlon, File) 0Comment Share topics: Measles MMR Vaccination Kern County Thanksgiving Travel Airborne Virus CDC Pediatricians BAKERSFIELD, Calif. (KBAK/KBFX) — As families prepare to hit the road for Thanksgiving, local doctors in Kern County are urging people to double-check vaccination records after the county confirmed two travel-related measles cases in August. FILE - This undated image made available by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention on Feb. 4, 2015, shows an electron microscope image of a measles virus particle. (Cynthia Goldsmith/Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, via AP, File) Dr. William Franklin, an emergency room physician with Adventist Health, said unvaccinated travelers face higher risk of infection. “If you’ve not been immunized, your kids haven’t been immunized, they’re going to be at risk,” Franklin said. The warning comes as the United States has lost its measles elimination status for the first time since 2000, with more than 5,400 cases reported nationwide this year. Measles is highly contagious and can spread through the air, according to Dr. Fernando Fan, a general pediatrician with Kaiser Permanente. “It’s highly contagious because the virus can be in the room floating in the air. It’s an airborne virus,” Fan said. FILE - A nurse, prepares a combination measles, mumps and rubella vaccine for a patient at a pediatrics clinic in Easley, S.C., March 17, 2026. (AP Photo/Mary Conlon, File) Doctors said early symptoms can resemble a common cold, starting with fever, cough and a runny nose. Symptoms can progress to red, watery eyes and a rash that typically begins on the face. Powered By 10 Kern County doctors urge vaccine checks ahead of Thanksgiving travel amid measles risk Share Next Stay Health experts recommend calling a doctor before going in if measles is suspected. Vaccination remains the primary protection. The CDC says one dose of the MMR vaccine is about 93% effective against measles, and two doses are about 97% effective. A LaGrange County school is alerting families to possible exposure to measles. With holiday travel increasing, Fan said he expects more cases are possible locally. “It’s a matter of time, whether we see more cases in Kern County. It’s not if it’s gonna be when,” Fan said. 卡纳贝克县确诊 麻疹 (Rubeola) 病例 - moraminn.com

Confirmed Measles (Rubeola) Case in Kanabec County - moraminn.com
新闻简报根据卡纳贝克县社区卫生部门和明尼苏达州卫生部的报告,明尼苏达州卡纳贝克县发现了一例 麻疹(Rubeola)确诊病例。该病例于 2026 年 11 月 27 日得到确认,卫生当局目前正在努力识别和联系可能接触过的个人。没有报告其他病例、死亡或暴露情况,也没有明确说明风险级别。应对工作正在进行中,正在进行接触者追踪和调查以控制局势。本文重点介绍了地方卫生部门和国家机构之间为解决 暴发 问题而进行的合作。证据中没有提供有关暴发规模或具体预防措施的进一步细节。

美国明尼苏达州卡纳贝克县

规模、影响与风险
发现 1 例 麻疹 确诊病例。没有报告其他病例、死亡或接触情况。证据中没有明确说明风险级别。
应对、状态与不确定性
社区卫生工作人员正在与 MDH 合作,以确定并联系可能接触过病毒的人。调查仍在进行中,没有提供有关回应的更多细节。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Confirmed Measles (Rubeola) Case in Kanabec County - moraminn.com

News Brief
A confirmed measles (Rubeola) case has been identified in Kanabec County, Minnesota, according to a report by Kanabec County Community Health and the Minnesota Department of Health. The case was confirmed on November 27, 2026, and the health authorities are currently working to identify and contact individuals who may have been exposed. No additional cases, deaths, or exposures were reported, and the risk level was not explicitly stated. The response is ongoing, with contact tracing and investigation being conducted to manage the situation. The article highlights the collaboration between local health departments and state agencies to address the outbreak. No further details on the scale of the outbreak or specific prevention measures were provided in the evidence.
Original source text (English)

What does the U.S. losing measles elimination status mean for California? - CalMatters
新闻简报本文讨论了美国失去 麻疹 淘汰地位对加州的影响。报告强调,今年该州确诊了60例麻疹病例,超过了前两年总数的总和。这一事态发展引起了人们对公共卫生和 疫苗接种 比率的担忧,特别是考虑到更广泛的国家背景。这篇文章强调需要提高警惕并采取有针对性的干预措施,以防止进一步传播。但文章并未提供具体的应对措施或风险评估细节,使得该州麻疹的现状存在一定的不确定性。焦点仍然是失去消除状态的影响以及加州卫生系统面临的潜在挑战。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

What does the U.S. losing measles elimination status mean for California? - CalMatters

News Brief
The article discusses the implications of the U.S. losing its measles elimination status for California. It highlights that the state confirmed 60 measles cases this year, surpassing the total from the previous two years combined. This development raises concerns about public health and vaccination rates, especially in light of the broader national context. The piece emphasizes the need for increased vigilance and targeted interventions to prevent further spread. However, the article does not provide specific details on the response measures or risk assessments, leaving some uncertainty about the current status of measles in the state. The focus remains on the impact of the loss of elimination status and the potential challenges ahead for California’s health system.

泛美卫生组织:美国、墨西哥、玻利维亚失去 麻疹 消除地位 - Medscape

PAHO: US, Mexico, Bolivia Lose Measles Elimination Status - Medscape
新闻简报文章报道称,经泛美卫生组织 (PAHO) 证实,美国、墨西哥和玻利维亚已失去 麻疹 消除状态。这意味着麻疹在这些国家不再缺席,表明该疾病卷土重来。该文章没有提供具体的病例数、死亡人数或暴露人数,但强调了这一发展的影响。消除状态的丧失表明各国可能需要重新评估其公共卫生策略以控制 麻疹 的传播。这篇文章强调了解决这个问题对于未来重新获得消除地位的重要性。该信息基于著名医学新闻来源 Medscape 于 2026 年 11 月 26 日发布的新闻报道。
查看新闻五要素
时间
据 2026 年 11 月 26 日发表的文章报道,美国、墨西哥和玻利维亚已失去 麻疹 淘汰状态。
地点与对象
美国、墨西哥和玻利维亚是受到 麻疹 消除状态丧失影响的国家。
事件
正如 PAHO(泛美卫生组织)所证实并在文章中报道的那样,美国、墨西哥和玻利维亚已经失去了 麻疹 消除状态。
规模、影响与风险
失去消除状态表明麻疹在这些国家不再缺席,但文章没有提供具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露数。
应对、状态与不确定性
文章讨论了消除状态的丧失以及恢复消除状态的可能性,但没有提供当前调查或应对措施的详细信息。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

PAHO: US, Mexico, Bolivia Lose Measles Elimination Status - Medscape

News Brief
The article reports that the United States, Mexico, and Bolivia have lost their measles elimination status, as confirmed by the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO). This means that measles is no longer absent in these countries, indicating a resurgence of the disease. The article does not provide specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers, but it highlights the implications of this development. The loss of elimination status suggests that the countries may need to reassess their public health strategies to control the spread of measles. The article emphasizes the importance of addressing the issue to potentially regain elimination status in the future. The information is based on a news report published on 2026-11-26 by Medscape, which is a reputable medical news source.
